the report i have the honour to present to you today is an opinion on the commission's annual report on state aid in the european union, for which the community has competence under articles 87, 88 and 89 of the treaties. the commission report is essentially a descriptive report describing the development of state aid in the manufacturing sector and other sectors, according to different typologies, such as the method of financing and the objectives pursued. i would refer to the explanatory statement for the quantitative part of the report and would simply point out that the average annual amount for the period under review is around eur 95 billion, which represents a decrease of around 13% compared with the period 1993-95, a decrease which is mainly due to a reduction in aid in the federal republic of germany. clearly, the level of declared state aid is, on average, broadly stable over the period covered and amounts to about 1.2% of community gdp, which is, coincidentally, roughly the equivalent of the community budget for one year. however, differences between countries are considerable and can be measured in different ways, in particular as a percentage of value added and per employee. i think it is also interesting to add state aid and community interventions which can be equated in some ways with state aid. it is therefore clear that the four countries which benefit from the cohesion fund, among others, are at the top of the ranking. i come now to the proposals in the report. firstly, we note that the commission considers that the data, as presented in the commission's annual report, are too aggregated to allow an in-depth assessment of state aid policy, which is both legitimate, sensitive to national interests and quite important from the point of view of respect for competition, under the very provisions of the treaty. the commission can only collect and analyse data provided by the member states. it is therefore up to the states and regions to ensure the quality of the data provided, and our committee believes that further efforts must be made in this area. it is in the same spirit that the idea of a public register of state aid, accessible via the internet, is being defended by our parliamentary committee. better and more detailed data, particularly in terms of objectives pursued and results achieved, should enable the european commission to carry out studies on the socioeconomic evaluation of national and regional state aid policies itself or to arrange for such studies to be carried out on a regular basis. and in so far as some of these studies already exist, to make its own comments more openly known, in view of the objectives of the treaties, which are not only to ensure the competitiveness of the european economy, but also sustainable development and economic and social cohesion. by emphasizing the quality of the information provided, our debate in committee and therefore the report i have the honour to present to you, avoided the simplistic view that the amount of state aid is either too high or too low in absolute terms. a majority of the committee members have been more concerned with finding a balance between the need for the states and companies to enforce the rules of competition and the recognition of the value of such aid in contributing to the objectives of the treaty, in particular, as i have already said, in the areas of sustainable development, research and development and economic and social cohesion. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the adverse effects of ionizing radiation. seven amendments are tabled for this plenary session. most of them reflect in fact differences in our policies on the appropriateness and effectiveness of state aid, given the shortcomings, whether acknowledged or not, of private investment alone, market failures or market inadequacies. there is one amendment, i should like to stress, concerning the energy sector, which seems to me as rapporteur particularly important. i would like to conclude this presentation, commissioner, by stressing on the one hand a concern of the members of the committee and on the other hand a requirement of our committee. the concern is with the pre-accession process of the countries of central and eastern europe from the point of view of competition policy and state aid. this is certainly a complex issue and we would like to hear from the commission on the current state of play, particularly in terms of the ability of the accession economies to comply with the competition rules and, as far as state aid is concerned, the need, probably, for specific state aid rules to help restructure their sectors. finally, i would like to conclude by saying that the requirement concerns the future competence of the european parliament in the areas we are discussing, competition policy and state aid, with a view to the intergovernmental conference. our report, as you know, mr commissioner, advocates the idea of the codecision procedure for basic state aid legislation.
- (nl) mr president, by definition a white paper is not to be taken or left, but is intended to provoke reactions. from this point of view, the white paper is a success. it is a good basis for discussion and should therefore be welcomed. i understand the authors' initial views and i agree with them. i also assume that you, commissioner, want to honour your reputation and the work of your predecessors, and that your services share the same objective. i cannot imagine that the commission would take initiatives which would result in a departure from european integration and in renationalisation. i am, however, concerned and have questions. i wonder first about the coherent application of the policy. i am a strong supporter of cultural diversity in general, but not of the culture of competition in the internal market. the internal market requires a competition policy which is uniform not only in its concept but also in its implementation. european regulations are indeed enacted and communications on their interpretation are actually transmitted. the commission should also have a right of appeal and should provide guidelines to national competition authorities. i wonder, however, whether we are not running the risk of ending up in an echternach procession, where for every two steps forward, we take one step back. i would therefore like to know a little more about how the commission intends to ensure in practice the uniform application of the proposed policy and whether you consider that the paths outlined from point 100 of the white paper onwards can be followed in a realistic way. secondly, i understand the concerns of companies about legal certainty. many cases are brought in this context. but this instrument will disappear in the future. you say in the white paper that the commission will issue some individual provisions which can serve as guidelines, but what will be your criteria for granting and refusing such special arrangements? thirdly, i would like to know whether the commission has considered what the consequences of its new approach to business strategy would be. i am particularly concerned about the fate of small and medium-sized enterprises, which will lose some of the legal and economic protection they enjoy, as is the case with the new block exemption in the distribution sector. fourthly, i would like to know why the commission does not opt for the application of the sanction which annuls acts which are manifestly contrary to the competition rules. fifthly, there is the forthcoming enlargement and i wonder whether the applicant countries will be able to play along. they're still in the training phase. what guarantees do we have that they will develop into first-class players in the internal market? sixthly and lastly, i would like to recall a point which i have already raised in my report on vertical restraints, namely the legal privilege of corporate lawyers. if the commission implements its plans set out in the white paper, it seems to me that discrimination in the internal market and between internal and external legal advice will become greater and therefore less permissible. does the commission intend to take any action to give legal privilege to in-house lawyers in all member states? commissioner, i am asking these questions as a defender of the internal market and i hope that in this sense we are all partners here so that the debate between genuine partners does not remain sterile but is, on the contrary, fruitful.

- mr president-in-office of the council, you have by a happy coincidence inherited from the calendar a special responsibility: to set on track an intergovernmental conference which is clearly crucial for the future of europe. in addition - and this will be the second subject i will address - you will also be chairing a conference, the importance of which is obvious to all, on employment and colleagues who spoke rightly stressed the inadequacy of what had happened in helsinki. we do not think that such a narrow agenda is acceptable. we clearly want at least four concrete items to be included in the agenda: the legal status of the charter of fundamental rights and its implications for citizenship; enhanced cooperation, an essential mechanism; the progressive integration of foreign and security policy, with a particular focus on conflict prevention in the community pillar; finally, the revision of the obsolete euratom treaty, which must be reviewed in its objectives and incorporated into the treaty on union. the intergovernmental approach has clearly shown its limits in terms of method, and has long since shown its limits with its corollary, the rule of unanimity. the council has wrongly chosen not to change its method. we are disappointed, but if the council is to accept the thesis that we want a more democratic and stronger union, it is imperative at the very least to involve the european parliament as widely as possible, and i would add the national parliaments. the european parliament must be involved, as must the commission, because we are the two institutions with a community vision, and it would be logical for it to have a consistent opinion procedure at the end of the negotiations. if not, i fear, as do other members, that the gap between public opinion and the european institutions will widen. we have had an important process in seattle, a strong reaction from civil society to a global institution that is not understood and whose functioning is perceived as dangerous for society as a whole. the european union should not have to go through the same process, and i would personally regret it. you'll soon be touring the capitals to convince your colleagues to expand the agenda and change the methodology. we shall await the results of your efforts before we decide on the future of this intergovernmental conference. finally, i would like to stress a point which i believe has not been sufficiently emphasized: the almost imperative need to make this conference a success, otherwise the european union will be permanently blocked. i am thinking of a subject which is very close to our hearts, the taxation issue. for several years now we have been unable to take ambitious decisions on environmental taxation because the rule of unanimity prevents us from doing so, and this brings me to the second point of my speech, namely employment, since this type of taxation also creates jobs. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. while it is clearly a factor in competitiveness, it should not be allowed to obscure a reality which is less bright and more worrying. the fact is that although innovation often plays an important role, competitiveness is sometimes achieved by dubious mechanisms: in some cases a significant deterioration in working conditions in europe, increased risks, deterioration of the environment and widespread stress. this is no empty phrase: a recent report by the european foundation in dublin shows that working conditions are bad or deteriorating for a significant number of workers; the 1999 report of the european environment agency on the environment indicates that 11 out of 12 parameters studied are either the status quo or deteriorating. the erika oil tanker disaster also shows that competitiveness in an economic sector can be achieved at the cost of worsening working conditions, increased risks and a serious threat to the environment. i therefore believe that it is time to revise some of the existing paradigms and i regret that this is not more clearly reflected in your document. for example, i think innovation can also go hand in hand with a search for an increase not in labour productivity but in the productivity of scarce and non-renewable resources. it is a path that is certainly a royal one, not only for sustainable development, but also for the creation of more stable, safer, less stressful and more promising jobs for the future. so we are back to where i started. all this can only be done if we change the way the european institutions work. this is a very important objective.

in writing. - the problem of storms in various european countries shows the fragility of our society, which is characterized by its economic and technological development. we realize that we are not immune to the disastrous effects of weather-related disasters such as floods, earthquakes, and tidal waves. some countries have been able to provide a solution to the problem of compensation for victims of such phenomena. france is one example, but it is a large country with significant resources. this is not the case in smaller countries. in belgium, for example, a project to compensate victims of natural disasters has been in the works for 10 years. it was at european level that the different projects clashed. firstly, although it was possible to impose prices and duties before the completion of the internal market, this is no longer the case and i think that is normal. on the other hand, it is more difficult to accept that it is not possible to provide for the creation of a mutual reinsurance fund to which companies operating on the belgian market would be obliged to insure themselves. secondly, the state guarantee could only be used if the system had exhausted all its funds (in the event of a major disaster). but our priority is to provide the victims with a total, unlimited guarantee. already, fate has fallen on them without their being responsible, should they be handicapped further by too little insurance cover? the state guarantee is essential for this, since no conventional reinsurer offers unlimited conditions. it is the only way to maintain a fair consumer price. the call for conventional reinsurance will have a regulatory effect at the global level, which means that belgian insured persons will be paying for hurricanes in central america somewhere... the idea is not to isolate ourselves, but to organize a fair system that is not subject to the laws of the market. i would add that this is the only way for the state to ensure that the system operates in the public interest, by keeping an eye on the accumulation and use of technical provisions. if this "right of sight" is not allowed, the state will never agree to give its guarantee. a controlled reinsurance mechanism is the only one which avoids a selection of risks by the natural laws of the market. if a mechanism of national solidarity is not guaranteed by a system which derogates, even temporarily, from the laws of the market, bad risks will be excluded, whereas it is precisely these that we are trying to protect (e.g. river-side dwellings). that is why i am raising the question of a derogation from european law, from the traditional competition law. this is an idea that is in line with the treaty. article 87 (2) authorises state aid 'to make good the damage caused by natural disasters'. the likelihood of such a disaster happening again is far from unlikely. solutions still need to be found for the financing of compensation for victims of natural disasters. a temporary derogation from competition law would be a hope of solving this distressing problem in a country like belgium.

commission (en) - first of all, i would like to say that i am delighted to present this communication on the application of the precautionary principle, which was drafted in collaboration with david byrne and erkki liikanen. the precautionary principle is not a new concept. the community has been using it for some time now in a whole range of policies relating to the environment and human, animal and plant health. this is explicitly mentioned in the environmental provisions of the ec treaty since maastricht. it also appears in a number of international texts such as the rio declaration and, more recently, the protocol on biosafety. the essence of the precautionary principle is clear. this is about taking action in a particular policy area when there is no scientific consensus and when there is good reason to believe that the potential risks are high enough to make action necessary. however, the use of the precautionary principle has come under increasing scrutiny in recent years. events such as the bse crisis and the dioxin crisis have encouraged a growing public debate on the circumstances in which precautionary action is justified and necessary. in the light of this growing interest, the commission therefore considered it useful to present a communication to define its position on the use of the precautionary principle in the context of the communication has two basic purposes. it aims to explain in a clear and coherent manner how the commission applies and intends to apply the precautionary principle in its risk management and to present guidelines for its application which are based on sound and coherent principles. we also hope that this communication will contribute to a better common understanding of how to manage risks and to allay fears that the precautionary principle could be used arbitrarily or could constitute a disguised form of trade protectionism. in applying the precautionary principle, the commission is guided by the need to ensure a high level of protection of the environment and of human, animal and plant health. of course, this objective cannot be used to justify irrational or arbitrary measures, but it does imply that action can always be taken even in situations where there is no scientific consensus. as such, while the precautionary principle does not include the politicization of science, as some have claimed, it does take us to the intersection of science and politics. the initial decision to apply the precautionary principle depends largely on the level of protection sought and the level of risk that policy makers are willing to accept for society. so he is political by nature. however, the measures that can be taken subsequently must obviously respect the general principles applicable to risk management, and the guidelines on the application of the precautionary principle are therefore the key element of the document. the measures taken must be proportionate to the level of protection chosen - which means that we must not use a hammer and chisel to break a nut. the application of these measures must be non-discriminatory, which means that these measures must not make any difference, regardless of geographical origin. they must be consistent with similar measures already taken. thus, if a product has been approved, similar products must also be approved. measures are based on an examination of the potential benefits and costs of action or inaction. the measures taken must therefore be cost-effective and must be reviewed in the light of new scientific data. they must therefore also clearly define who is responsible for producing the scientific evidence needed for a more comprehensive risk assessment - the burden of proof. all these elements must apply cumulatively. it is also important to bear in mind that there is a wide range of measures that can be taken in applying the precautionary principle, such as a research programme, public information campaigns, recommendations, etc. the application of the precautionary principle does not therefore automatically imply a ban. this communication does not claim to be the final word on the matter. however, this is the first time that the commission has provided a structured presentation of this principle and its operational use. by presenting in detail how the commission applies and intends to apply the precautionary principle, we hope to be able to clarify the situation at community level and to contribute to the debate taking place at european and international level.
commission. - thank you very much, mrs dagmar roth-behrendt, for these questions. of course, all this is shared by the commission as a whole. it is a joint communication written by david byrne, erkki liikanen and myself, but it was adopted unanimously by the commission today. it was strongly supported by the other commissioners and was the subject of inter-service consultation throughout the commission. we have therefore worked extensively on this document and i am confident that everyone is able to describe the guidelines and principles set out in this document. yes, i would say that we already use this method of working for various complex issues, such as strategy for new chemicals. i am also sure that we will make it clear when we are going to use this principle. recently, we have used the precautionary principle to ban phthalates in pvc toys. of course, we apply it in various environmental sectors. this principle has been used mainly in the environmental field, but, of course, it has also been used in human health. so we will try to be very clear about how and when it is appropriate to use it.
- (nl) mr president, madam commissioner, on the problem of the relationship between science and the precautionary principle, the communication states that the precautionary principle always requires a political decision when scientific evidence is lacking. in doing so, we are placing a significant amount of power in the hands of the scientific community. the question is, what kind of scientists will provide this documentation and what will they defend? the communication also states that it is sufficient that there is a recognized minority of scientists to use the precautionary principle. i would like to know what is meant by a recognized minority. how do you describe her ? what is a recognized minority? as regards the political decision, i would also like to ask who is making the political decision. is that the commission? is this the council ? how much of a role can the european parliament play in this process? what is the role of the scientific committees? and what will be the next role of the food authorities that you will set up? finally, will this decision-making be made in a transparent and public manner? those are my three questions.

- (nl) mr president, we are in favour of liberalizing postal services in the european union. this liberalization must first of all mean that customers will be better served. this is the direction, this must be the final objective. liberalisation began because there were doubts about the structure of postal services, about the value of a monopoly. is a monopoly flexible enough in a market where customer demand can change very rapidly and where the market situation can also change very rapidly, because, for example, electronic information media would be used much more extensively? the question is therefore: what requirements should be imposed on a monopoly or on a part of the services controlled by a monopoly ? on this point most member states have always refused to move forward and i am pleased to see that in my country the new government is taking up a matter which should have been dealt with long ago and that the belgian post office and the belgian government are now taking the necessary steps to ensure that the belgian post office is, on the one hand, ready to support competition by 2003. as a new member of parliament, i am trying to familiarize myself with the matter and i must frankly say that i have a lot of problems with it. i consulted a series of studies which were commissioned at the time by the european commission, but at the same time i find documents from post-europe which state that these studies are not correct and that the basis on which they were carried out is wrong. i think that the commission could have at least been a little more active on this point and could have given us a little more information. she could also have given us the studies she had promised us and which we had also requested. as far as i know, that has not yet been the case. i therefore ask you, commissioner, to help a new member like myself in this difficult task. like mr miller, i would greatly appreciate it if you could explain to us in the coming months or weeks, and if possible in the coming days, how the 1997 directive is being implemented. secondly, what is the state of progress of the universal service, how much does it cost, how is it organised, how do you intend to organise it, what proposals can you make? i would like to receive more specific information on this. apparently, the post office is a very powerful lobby and rightly so. hundreds of thousands of people make a living at the post office. in addition, as mr miller also pointed out, my country will not be wiped off the map, despite its population density, if the postman does not pass by every day. however, we have a large number of people in our homes, elderly people for example, who only see one person in their day and it is the postman. let us, then, also endeavor to take it into account. the post office is much more than just a place to deliver parcels and letters. for companies too, the post is particularly important. a study shows that 90% of mail traffic is between businesses, compared with 10% between individuals. what do you want to liberalize? i would like to be better informed about this, because the most important question is how you can best serve the customer with the services that exist. there are postal companies which have a monopoly and still work in a very valuable way. we must protect ourselves against such things and denounce other practices.
- (nl) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the forthcoming liberalization of the traditional national postal services raises the inevitable question of what the postal services will in future regard as their basic services, in other words, what the postal services will still be doing themselves, what they will be prepared to do together at international level and what they will no longer be prepared to do. this question must be answered in view of the fact that the empirical monopoly has already been eroded in practice by all kinds of mail companies and by technological developments such as fax and e-mail. on the other hand, employment is at the heart of the liberalization of postal services. past experience has sufficiently demonstrated that linear measures of dismissal and incentives to voluntary departure have often led to undesirable effects. i remember that in the case of the belgian telephone network, retired employees had to be recalled to prevent too much of the know-how and experience from leaving the company. the subject of the discussions in the liberalization of postal services will again undoubtedly be the reduction of staff. in this respect, however, it must be borne in mind that the incentives to voluntary departure are mainly for people who are already working under pressure or who will be able to find work easily elsewhere; in other words, the people the company in question particularly needs. in my view, the commission would be showing a well-balanced management of human resources if it could dispel a number of these concerns so that the liberalisation of a sensitive sector such as the postal service can take place in as peaceful a social environment as possible.

- (nl) mr president, recent events, especially the sinking of the erika, have shown that our coasts are constantly threatened by maritime disasters. we have already had a debate in strasbourg on the prevention of such disasters and it has proved essential to draw up a european plan to ensure maritime safety. in addition to prevention, we must also look at the consequences of such disasters, and first and foremost at the ecological impacts. several hundred kilometres of french coastline are polluted. thousands of fish and birds died. it is possible that some habitats may be permanently affected. ecological restoration is often a long-term process. the current system of capped compensation only for quantifiable environmental damage is inadequate. we must quickly move to a system of compensation without a ceiling, which would also be used for environmental damage that cannot be quantified. the 'polluter pays' principle must be applied consistently. i am in favour of a zero tolerance policy on marine pollution, which requires stricter and more effective controls. however, we must also consider the economic consequences. i am in favour of a european recovery plan for the sectors concerned, from fisheries to the hornet sector and from mytiline farming to oyster farms; in short, an emergency plan to be applied in the event of a maritime accident. those who are victims of the unbridled pursuit of profit by others deserve our solidarity, especially in a transition phase. i would like to address the health aspect. belgian volunteers also sacrificed their christmas holidays to clean up beaches and save birds and fish, often with their bare hands. i was surprised to read in the newspaper that mud could be carcinogenic. i was even more surprised to read that the french authorities concerned had known about this for days and had not bothered to ask the volunteers to take protective measures. i would like the commissioner to tell me whether the european commission has been informed of the possible risk to public health and if so, when did it receive this information? mr president, we must learn from the past. maritime disasters such as the erika may occur again tomorrow along the dutch, english, spanish or belgian coasts. the regulation we are drawing up in europe will be seen as a precedent. so let us be tough on the polluters and generous to the victims.

- mr. chairman of the council, do you know how archimedes expounded his theory of leverage? he said, "give me a foothold, and i'll lift the world". clearly, in lisbon you are trying to upset the world, or at least a world that is ours: that of europe. as for leverage, you have described all the mechanisms of it through your exciting proposal, exciting in many ways. the point of support remains. the point of support is the means. but you are here in front of an assembly whose members are still living with the memory of what i shall call the white paper syndrome, that is to say, jacques delors's wonderful initiative which failed against the ecofin accountants. we do not want the same thing to happen to you today, because the project you are offering us is so exciting that it can give europe the dynamism and the future that our colleague mr goebbels has called for. but you still have to be able to afford it, the ability that i'm afraid you're a little short of today. so it's up to you to forge them. it is not the corset of agenda 2000 that will give you the necessary means. we must therefore encourage your colleagues to finance the proposals you are going to make to us. one example among others - and this is one of the important features of what you propose - is the information and knowledge society. it is clear - and you have made it clear - that social inequality is a fundamental problem which we must combat. not to mention what i'll call the digital fracture. indeed, to whom are you going to propose this information and knowledge society? the problem is that we are faced with a population that is getting older and older, a population that is still poor and a population that is unfortunately getting less and less well-educated. so it is from the bottom up that we must act if we do not want this knowledge society to be reduced to the race for the profits of a few successful, necessary start-ups. but that's not your goal. in the system you have provided, there is one thing missing, which i regret, and that is the public services. in the history of our european social model, public services have played a key role. today, it is true, the time is no longer for monopolies and the commission has started a process of liberalization, of putting a number of public sectors in competition in order to modernise them and make them more efficient. but we can't stop there. in the context of competition and the information society, it is now necessary to define better what the main public service missions of the future will be for public enterprises, but also for private enterprises in this sector. we need stronger initiatives in this respect, which are not yet on the commission's work programme but which you could give a boost to: that would, i think, be an extremely important signal. finally, this knowledge society is based on research and development and i would like to welcome the commission's initiative to create a european research area. but, mr president, on this point, the commission and parliament will need the council to provide the necessary means for the creation of this european research area. the framework programme will not be enough, it will have to be supplemented, its main guidelines will have to be changed. it's a big job, but we're counting on you.

commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank you for this serious, practical and simple debate. i would just ask you to consider my speech as part of a larger discourse that we have day after day. the technique of the short speeches which parliament has chosen obliges us to consider them as a whole, including the speeches made yesterday and a month ago. i repeat, just as we were synthetic last month in presenting the broad lines, we were analytical today because the annual programme must be analytical. the essential role of our cooperation is increasingly apparent in this debate. all the subjects discussed this morning - the need for close coordination of the budget, joint action and a position on the intergovernmental conference and relations with the council - require joint work between parliament and the commission. the way we approach the next intergovernmental conference is important because it is precisely there that the rules for the future will be determined, since there will be no other rules immediately after it. we cannot keep organizing it indefinitely. someone today expressed some unease about the long list. well, i think today was the time for enumeration and analysis. it is true that some chapters are missing from this list. many of you have rightly noted the absence of the tourism chapter, which, as has been said, concerns more people than agriculture. we must of course be attentive to this aspect, even if, as you know, the commission's powers in this area are rather limited. in any case, the list contains many subjects whose details will have to be studied in the committees. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work is a very important one. so we have a whole series of problems to solve together this year. we have, however, also a great opportunity, which many of you have fortunately mentioned, namely the possibility of having a healthy economy for at least two or three years. we have no choice but to exploit this opportunity, since we have highlighted a serious budgetary problem. if we take account of the speeches made in parliament today, the result is a precise but also ruthless analysis. as far as agriculture is concerned, we cannot renounce existing resources, which are considered to be a priority for the survival of certain rural areas, but on the other hand we have duties in the field of foreign policy such as kosovo and others, and this leads to incompatibilities in our budget. we will partially remedy this - as mr elles has said - by an economy which we hope will grow by more than 3% a year and thus bring in more funds, but we know that these resources will not be enough to ensure the compatibility of the programmes and objectives mentioned in this morning's debate. relations between parliament and the commission are becoming essential in making these decisions. the time has come for the truth, the time for us to see together what our future will be made of, that is to say, to define the budgetary limits, the fundamental decisions to be taken and the actions to be taken. we have on the one hand the fundamental subject which we will have to deal with in the coming months and on the other the problem, which some of you have mentioned, of competences, of governance, of the european constitution, namely the powers of the countries, the regions, the länder. i'm fully aware of that fact. i met last week with the official representatives of the german länder to discuss this problem and the discourse did not dwell on decentralisation but went much deeper. we have talked about the levels of governance to be achieved in europe and not about decentralisation issues. that is why i have insisted on the need for a white paper on governance. we're inventing something new. our actions may be criticized, they may have limits, they may have difficulties, but we raise the intellectual and experimental barrier of politics. to do this, we must work together. the white paper on governance and the problems of budgetary compatibility are the two pillars on which we must develop common political action for the near future. i can assure you of only one thing: the method used by the commission really starts from a white board - not a black board - on which we have to write what we think can be beneficial to the institutions for which we are responsible. of course, we must be diligent when we are not confined to making large projects. this is the third request i have received, the one on the report on our activities. we present an annual report on the actions carried out. we presented the 1999 action plan in february. we want to go into it more deeply, but it already covers all the actions and we proposed to work on better information on current work. this is the project of a great european union, a project with a definite objective. if we do not do this, we will not find our place in the new world order. someone here has criticized globalization. i think that criticism is unnecessary because globalization is real and the problem is how to manage it. do we want to be the leaders of this globalization? do we want to manage it responsibly towards the developing countries and the third world? i think that's a really important strategy. we cannot afford to ignore seattle, but must resume the negotiations immediately, trying, of course, or rather wanting to, to remedy the mistakes which have caused them to fail. we feel the need for a global conscience, for a global protection, which goes beyond our own personal interests. let us be careful not to slow down globalization, because that is the only hope for india, for china, for the countries that have to assert themselves in this global economy. we cannot imagine that we can stifle these countries at such a delicate time. this is our destiny, and it is our duty to create a europe which can play a role. enlargement is part of this overall process. it is not just a tool for peace-keeping, it is a tool for defining our role. the commission's position on enlargement is strong and courageous, but also severe and serious, because the objections are twofold. on the one hand, we want enlargement to take place immediately, which is also our wish, but on the other hand we want to clarify the consequences of enlargement and the instruments which will make it possible. when i said that we should proceed with enlargement by reassuring public opinion in the candidate countries and in our own countries, i was heard with perplexity in some of the candidate countries. but i said that in their interests and not just in ours, because if there were any suspicion that enlargement was merely superficial, we would create a public opinion opposed to this historic movement, to this event which will mark our action and give a moral meaning to the policy we are pursuing. we must therefore ensure in the budget, in enlargement and in governance that balance, that sense of mission, that sense of compatibility which guides the actions of all parliaments and all governments. (applause) i would like to thank
the rapporteur. - mr president, i think it is worth recalling that we voted on this proposal for a directive on cocoa and chocolate products at first reading in october 1997. in reality, therefore, it took the council of ministers a long time to reach agreement on a common position, since it was not until october 1999 that this common position was available. it has taken two years, and i think that a certain weariness has taken hold of the public, particularly in this parliament, to the extent that a number of members - for the sake of correctness, i will say that they are even in the majority in the committee on the environment - think that this is a balanced compromise. the common position is a balanced compromise and should not be touched too much. i personally believe that parliament's first reading vote was a balanced compromise and i deeply regret that the european commission has absolutely not supported parliament's position in the council's negotiations. at no time did the commission pass on parliament's amendments, and in fact, during the debate in the committee on the environment, the commission again decided not to touch on this common position: in other words, to reject all the amendments. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has adopted two important amendments. i welcome that, although i do not think it is enough. i think that this is not an acceptable compromise and that the use of vegetable fats has been trivialised by not providing consumers with sufficient information, by ignoring the problem of the unreliability of the analytical method, by removing subsidiarity and by responding to the demands of the chocolate multinationals, while ignoring the concerns of the chocolate manufacturers and smes, the rights of consumers and the future of millions of cocoa farmers. i am thinking in particular of the more than ten million cocoa farmers in west africa whose future is clearly in jeopardy. why do i say that when an annex proposed in the common position seems to solve the problem? there is indeed an annex which limits the list of authorized vegetable fats to six substances, including shea butter, which is a harvest product from some poor african countries, including mali and burkina faso. unfortunately, this limitation does not guarantee the future. on the one hand, it is possible to produce cocoa butter equivalents without shea butter, and i have no doubt that for reasons of cost and product reliability, such solutions are being sought. on the other hand, industrial, chemical or genetic engineering processes make it possible to obtain cocoa butter equivalents with a low-cost base substance that is one of the six selected: palm oil from industrial crops, whose market price can be ten times lower than that of cocoa butter. by opposing the producers of shea butter, who deserve our attention, to the producers of cocoa, the promoters of vegetable fats, with the support of the european commission, which i regret very much, they are hiding a reality which i consider to be beyond dispute: the only beneficiaries of the entry into force of the text proposed by the council in its present form are the chocolate multinationals, whose objective is to increase their profits by favouring cheap vegetable fats and by increasing the downward pressure on the price of cocoa. i therefore believe that a number of amendments tabled by colleagues from various political groups, including mrs thomas-mauro, mrs ries and mrs isler béguin, are likely to restore parliament's position adopted at first reading and give the text more weight. i would mention in particular the mandatory indication on the main side of the product of the presence of vegetable fats; the requirement of prior validation of a reliable method of analysis; the principle of subsidiarity, which was abolished by the council, although originally proposed by the commission; the rejection of genetic engineering and chemical methods - i would point out that the committee on the environment supports the rejection of the use of genetic engineering; the commission has already made a number of proposals, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. that, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, seems to me to be the approach we voted for two years ago. it is true that it is contrary to the council's common position, but i believe that parliament's role is to exist in the co-decision process and not to bow to the council's decisions, even if they have been acquired after a very long delivery, which lasted two years.
- (nl) mr president, chocolate was the subject of a heated debate at first reading. and rightly so, and not without result. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the quality of the product seemed to be of secondary importance and the commission was not particularly concerned about the ethical and legal obligations towards developing countries. the debate was not without results. anyone who analyses the common position in depth must admit that democracy really works. we have not resigned ourselves to complaints, but have actively taken amendments. allow other laws ? yes, if necessary, but then on our terms. we wanted only tropical fats, the common position lists them in a restrictive way. we wanted double labelling, and the common position allows it, although we had preferred that the additional information should be shown on the front of the packaging. we had asked for an impact study. we shall have it two and a half years after the directive enters into force. we demanded that the commission submit a proposal for amendment. well, it must submit to it and we can, if necessary, use our right of amendment again. we did not want to have a comitology for essential amendments. we have obtained codecision. and we had asked for more precise methods of analysis; i think the research institute in ispra did a good job. like many members, we were not satisfied with the commission's proposal. we have not been able to prevent the introduction of non-cocoa fats, but we have been able to limit their use in such a way that the developing countries themselves recognize that the current text is finally acceptable and that 'quality chocolate' containing only cocoa can be distinguished from other types of chocolate. mr president, as the epp group's counter-rapporteur, i am proud that almost all the amendments from the first reading, which have so strongly influenced the common position, are the product of our handiwork. no further amendments were therefore tabled on behalf of the epp. however, some colleagues will still support amendments proposed by others, especially since these are the amendments which we proposed at first reading and which have been taken up. even if no amendments are adopted, the result is still visible. i would like to conclude with the following reflection. in the debate on chocolate, the word ethics was mentioned several times. in my view, ethical behaviour also implies that if this house succeeds, we should also be able to say so and dare to take action. when you win a victory, you should also be able to enjoy it. perhaps we too should start learning it, ladies and gentlemen.
- (nl) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the common position on the directive on cocoa and chocolate products is - and i quote the chocolate industry lobby quite literally - a directive which creates a genuine single market for chocolate products, respecting national traditions in the field of chocolate, providing consumers with complete information and safeguarding the interests of developing countries. but is that really the case? i shall start with consumer information. the commission itself has admitted that there is no sufficiently reliable method of analysis. there is a margin of error of 2%. of the 5%, this represents an increase of 50%. that seems unacceptable to me. moreover, the control depends on the type of chocolate and the information the producer wishes to give. we are not serious, either with ourselves or, above all, with the consumer, when we introduce a directive which we are not in a position to control effectively. the third world interests: the fact that this argument is constantly put forward by both supporters and opponents of the common position is a matter of serious concern to me. some say that the directive has positive effects on third world countries, while others argue that more than one million west african farmers will be adversely affected. what about now and what about then? is it too much to ask to check, control and analyse in advance what the consequences of this directive will be, for example, for exports and the sustainable development of cocoa-producing countries? finally, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to raise another point which is very close to my heart. it is rightly said, and wrongly so, that this parliament cannot adopt any amendments, because otherwise this fragile compromise would be jeopardized. well, if that's the case and if that's an argument, i suggest we close our books now. each common position is always a delicate balance between national interests, ecological requirements and social considerations. the european parliament must also play its part in this matter and ensure that the common position is amended on a number of points and that we get a directive with positive effects for both consumers and the third world.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, after 25 years of debate, we are here again to discuss chocolate, a very tasty name and a very controversial subject. it is a question of harmonization, of taste, of custom, almost of culture. in short, chocolate divides and the cuts are not ideological or even national, however some may think. what is the ideological point, i ask you, in defending the cocoa-producing countries? we're in the field of ethics here. from a social point of view, we are talking about millions of families who live off cocoa production. we have formal commitments to them. then why the delay ? sixty-six months for the completion of an impact study of this directive: this period will be sufficient to cause irreparable damage to the export structures of these countries. this impact must be known beforehand, this is obvious. moreover, if there is to be harmonisation, for a consumer who is more demanding than ever on the quality of what he eats and who, by the way, has asked for nothing and has nothing to gain from this, the minimum minimorum, in my view, would be to qualify only 100% cocoa chocolate, real chocolate, as quality or any other label of excellence. any further harmonization will lead to a leveling-down, and that is not the europe the consumer wants.
- (nl) mr president, i am belgian and yet i am not against the common position. if the chocolate i love most were in danger, if the tradition of belgian chocolate makers were in danger, i would of course be a fervent opponent of this common position. this morning i bought a chocolate bar in strasbourg and the french chocolate seller told me very clearly what i had just bought. on the front of the product it was indicated: prestige noir intense 72% of cocoa and this, in gold letters, and a detail that was still very special for france, it was also indicated belgian chocolate. so i know what i bought. with the directive we will now have, i think it will be even easier for producers of 'quality chocolate' to distinguish themselves in a positive way from others if they wish to do so. i agree with the rapporteur on one point. cocoa bean farmers, small farmers in poor third world countries, are vulnerable. we must take this into account and that is why i am in favour of the commission submitting to us, at the latest two years after the directive enters into force, a study on the consequences of this directive. let us therefore let this matter settle itself and ensure that everyone can enjoy the chocolate they like and that a genuine internal market for chocolate is born.
- commissioner, you are walking on eggshells and i support your approach. with regard to the subsidiarity argument, i would simply say that i think it is also important to put forward the citizenship argument in these tax matters. it is unacceptable, after fifty years of european construction, that european citizens are not equal before the law simply because of the game of residents and non-residents. the notion of citizenship is therefore a common good, for which it is necessary to overcome failures such as the helsinki process. i would like to ask you three specific questions: first question: do you foresee that the system you propose can come into force in view of the integration of the financial markets and their necessary fiscal regulation ? second question: does the system you propose for environmental taxation provide for qualified majority voting? third question: would the mechanism you propose enable the council to put an end, by qualified majority, to the tax havens that exist within the european union?
- (nl) mr president, i can understand that some members think that citizens are sensitive to any european intrusion into tax and social matters. i can assure you that in my country many citizens are concerned that europe is doing nothing on tax and social matters because of this unanimity. i would therefore like to ask the commissioner two specific questions. the citizens who suffer the most serious consequences of this lack of intervention are those who wish to benefit from the free movement of persons, and in particular frontier workers. the files relating to the coordination of social security, enlargement to third countries and supplementary social security schemes have been blocked on the council's desk for years. can the commissioner tell me whether he is also referring to this case when he speaks of a qualified majority? secondly, i would like to join my colleagues in asking whether the dossiers on the prima rolo group table fall within the qualified majority envisaged by the commissioner.
- (nl) mr president, madam commissioner, i have no problem with the principle of the directive. it is an important instrument of environmental policy for the coming years. clear objectives were set for the member states. they know what they have to do. but if i look at the table of ceilings by member state, i have the impression that there are two sides to every story. i note that my country, belgium, has to reach the maximum scenario, which is therefore not economically viable, while some neighbouring regions as polluted as my country can apply a more lenient policy. for a large country can compensate for its bad rules with its better-looted regions. a small country can't do that. let me give you an example. according to your information and your maps, the north of france, which is as polluted and polluting as belgium, can deal with the problems at a lower level. for example, the implementation of this directive costs every belgian 103 euros a year, according to your calculations. a frenchman, and therefore a frenchman from the north of the country, pays only 15 euros and that is eight times less. that's because my country is smaller and surrounded by polluters. for my country, 1% of gross domestic product per year is too much. i therefore ask for support for amendments nos 16 and 17, in which i ask that the belgian government be allowed to review the situation on the basis of the annual reports.

my attention in the last few days, the number of suicides among intellectuals has increased dramatically in recent weeks. the example of afghanistan shows that discrimination against women is not a cultural but a political fact. afghanistan is not the only country where women's rights are trampled underfoot. millions of women worldwide are still being cut or married against their will. in many african countries, women cannot own property or open bank accounts without the consent of their husbands. other practices, such as polygamy and widowhood, have become intolerable in the aids era. it is no wonder that africa has more women infected with the aids virus. we must condemn all practices that lead to female mutilation or death. within the european union, circumcision must be punished. committed in our own countries. domestic violence is still a major problem in the european union. we must fight it with all our strength, including the use of criminal sanctions. the trafficking of women and girls for sexual exploitation is equally serious, with an estimated 500,000 trafficked each year to western europe. according to the undp, this "business" is worth $7 billion. a firm and the commission. i therefore ask both sides to make a tangible and concrete gesture to achieve a constructive result. in any case, we will judge these two cases by their actions, not by their words.

- (nl) madam president, by introducing amendments to the chocolate directive, we have tried to offer better protection to consumers who want to know whether they are being offered real chocolate or a product in which cocoa butter has been replaced by different fats. unfortunately, these amendments were not accepted. we therefore continue to be concerned about the cocoa farmers of the third world. despite the possible improvements for manufacturers of other vegetable fats, we expect the net result to be negative for the third world anyway. it is regrettable that the study on the impact of this new directive on the countries of the south was not carried out beforehand. chocolate will no longer be a product made entirely of cocoa butter. in some parts of the union, the name 'chocolate' was also used, even though the product already contained substitutes. we regret that the standardization is being carried out at the expense of consumers and cocoa producers and that it has been motivated by the lure of profit.
in writing. - the proposal for a directive on waste incineration is a complex and technical issue, and its implications, both environmental and economic, must be carefully weighed. at the end of the first reading, the european parliament had largely made its mark on the debate by proposing the merger of two directives on the incineration of household waste and the incineration of hazardous waste. today i think that the common position which the council has presented to us is a very good compromise, and i can agree with it. i would like to mention one particular point, however, namely co-incineration in cement works, and explain why i voted against two amendments. on the one hand, the first part of amendment no 25 to annex ii, which sets emission limit values for the cement industry, and in particular the value of 15 mg/m3 for dust. most member states currently have limit values of 50 mg/m3. in a recent cost-benefit analysis, the commission concluded that values of 30 mg/m3 are the best compromise. the imposition of 15 mg/m3 standards would imply excessive environmental costs and would not be cost-effective. on the other hand, amendment no 14, on the definition of municipal waste, reintroduces a definition of mixed municipal waste, whereas this is already provided for in article 3 (3). a misinterpretation of this article could lead to the exclusion of the use of municipal waste in cement kilns. however, this type of treatment is provided for in the waste management plans of several public authorities. this does not put me at odds with my environmental concerns, since co-incineration in cement works is not particularly harmful to the environment: cement kilns are subject to strict emission regulations. in addition, co-incineration is sometimes an integral part of the waste management policy of certain states or regions, under economically and environmentally acceptable conditions.
- (nl) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, if we are rightly taking the utmost care to do everything possible to achieve an information society for all, we must also do everything possible to avoid creating a two-speed society in the computer sector, with experts on one side and the ignorant on the other. i also attach great importance to the defence of small and mediumsized enterprises, especially small ones. in this respect, it is essential to ensure that safeguards are introduced in advertising. the absence of a harmonised policy on commercial communication may in fact create particularly insurmountable obstacles for small businesses. therefore, harmonisation of commercial communication is necessary. finally, i would also like to emphasize that the parties concerned, namely the consumer on the one hand and the entrepreneur on the other, must in any case be given the opportunity to agree in advance on the applicable court system. if the rule is that the consumer can always sue in his own court, this will become impossible for a small company in that it will have to obtain legal aid in the other 14 member states, which is of course quite inconceivable for small companies.
in writing. - (nl) we have approved the haarder report but would like to make the following remarks on paragraphs 19 and 20 of the draft report. paragraph 19 concerns the request to belgium for ratification of the charter for the protection of national minorities. in fact, flanders does not oppose belgium's ratification of the charter, provided that it is specified that it is not applicable in the belgian constitutional context. in the belgian federal state, the equality of the states is enshrined in the constitution: none of them constitutes a minority in the strict sense of the term. since the charter does not clearly define the concept of national minority, flanders considers that it is not applicable in the periphery either: in this context, according to flanders, the term 'national minority' can only refer to a minority established for centuries in a very specific territory. however, the french-speaking population has only been settled in the periphery for about 30 years and has refused to integrate as might have been expected (cf. the migration of the flemings to wallonia and the perfect integration of their descendants). moreover, the french-speaking population in the periphery enjoys linguistic facilities which provide protection, protection which real minorities can often ignore. any attempt to change the situation in the brussels suburbs by means of this charter implicitly calls into question the constitutional balances in belgium and once again demonstrates that a lack of knowledge of our federal system at european level must lead to the conclusion that it is preferable to have two separate member states, flanders and wallonia, rather than one single belgian federal member state. the french-speaking belgian members of parliament will no doubt continue to abuse this vote for small political games within belgium. we do not wish to play these games and we are clearly distancing ourselves from them. we also regret that a similar text has been included in paragraph 5 of the ludford report on racism and xenophobia. in the same vein, flanders maintains that the charter of minority languages, as pointed out in paragraph 20 of the haarder report, does not concern the situation of french in the brussels periphery but, for example, limburgish (admitted to the netherlands under the charter) or german in the arlon region of the province of luxembourg.

we also know that some member states are probably cooperating in their circumvention. i'm really very concerned. i believe that if our parliament wants to be the representative of the european citizens, it really has a duty to clarify this situation. we shall not do so if we are always waiting for initiatives from the council and the commission. i would remind you that 18 months ago we already voted a resolution which said: 'we ask that such surveillance technologies be the subject of a real open debate'. in the light of what i have just heard today, the debate is not really open. secondly, we said: 'we must adopt effective economic protection systems and encryption'. we are told the same thing today, 18 months later. are we going to vote for a new resolution in strasbourg that repeats the 1998 resolution? i really think it's outdated. therefore, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we must move to a higher gear and our parliament, as the third institution of the union, has the duty to be at the forefront of the fight.
