the rapporteur. - (nl) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, experience shows that scandals or crises can have a purifying effect and help to improve legislation. this is certainly the case for all legislation which directly or indirectly concerns food safety. there are many examples. it is now more than 20 years since the discovery of female sex hormones in baby food jars led directly to the adoption of the 1981/85 hormone directives, which were then re-adopted in 1988. similarly, the clenbuterol scandal, in which more than 15 000 calves were seized in north rhine-westphalia in 1988, led to the amendment in 1996 of the original hormones directives. in my own country, flanders, a series of attacks on slaughterhouse inspectors and the murder of the dedicated veterinarian karel van noppen have given rise to what i would safely call the strictest national legislation on hormones in europe. scandals can have a cleansing effect. we have the opportunity to see this today when we consider the amendment to directive 95/53 at second reading. because, at the heart of this amendment, there is once again a small scandal, namely the importation of citrus pulp with dioxin from brazil. this pulp is used as feed for dairy cows and this is how dioxin got into the milk for consumption. the authorities in baden-württemberg discovered this: they opened the case and found that in total, almost 106 000 tonnes of contaminated pulp were imported into the european union between december 1997 and february 1998. the scandal has shown us that the commission does not have sufficient means to intervene forcefully in an emergency. on 4 november 1998, at the request of the member states, the commission therefore submitted a proposal to amend the directive in question. on 16 december of the same year parliament had already approved this proposal without tabled any amendments. after the entry into force of the treaty of amsterdam, the commission immediately changed the legal basis from 100 a to 152. on 15 november last year the council adopted this common position. the council has made several drafting changes and, as the committee on the environment, we really do not see any problem with that. on the other hand, we do not entirely agree with the way in which the council prevents the commission from intervening directly in an emergency. now i have to be a little technical to deal with this complex issue of comitology legislation. since the commission's proposal was presented, the legislation on comitology has been amended and council decision 1999/468 is now in force. this legislation provides us with various procedures for transferring powers from the council to the commission. today we must concentrate on articles 5 and 6 of the council decision, which deal with the regulatory procedure and the safeguard procedure respectively. the commission's original proposal, as approved by parliament at first reading, was clearly drafted in the spirit of the current safeguard procedure. in its common position, the council now wants to do without it and wants the regulatory procedure to be used. however, everyone should be aware that under the latter procedure the commission cannot intervene freely and must always seek the council's authorization. after the citrus pulp scandal, the member states themselves asked for the commission to intervene. i think we must allow the commission to intervene quickly. the four amendments tabled are designed to ensure that article 6 of the legislation on comitology, and therefore the safeguard procedure, can be applied. the commission can therefore intervene directly in an emergency, for example by suspending the import of products which endanger human and animal health. the council then has 30 days to amend, confirm or even repeal the decision. if the council has not taken a decision within 30 days, the measures adopted by the commission shall be deemed to be repealed. i think this is a good procedure - the commission has never abused this kind of power - and it deserves our support in the council. we must also all know, ladies and gentlemen, that this is only a transitional stage. the same day that my report was approved by the committee on the environment, the european commission put forward a new proposal to amend directive 95/53/ec. she was quick to learn the lessons of the dioxin crisis. if parliament and the council follow the commission, a whole chapter will be devoted to the further development of the safeguard clause and animal feedingstuffs will also be placed under a 'fast' system. the commission has already made a number of proposals in the white paper on food safety. mr president, let us support the commission tomorrow by adopting the four amendments. let us go to war with the commission against the council and therefore opt for the conciliation procedure.
the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are dealing here with a second aspect of the problem of food security. the number of additives used in foodstuffs has increased over the years, particularly in connection with the industrialization of production. when i say more and more, just look at the numbers. the situation today shows that 307 additives are authorized in the european union. we are operating on the basis of the principle of a positive list. all additives on this list are authorised, those not so authorised are de facto prohibited. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment. i am thinking in particular of the problem of allergies. what are we having today? the commission has made nine proposals, either to add certain additives to the list or to add certain uses of additives already authorized. what are the criteria used in principle to accept an additive? there's three of them. the technological need, the consumer's utility and the safety. it must be admitted that over time, the technological need has always remained the dominant criterion, not that safety has been ignored, but the notion of safety has evolved a little over time. it is now common knowledge that the precautionary principle was virtually ignored seven or eight years ago, but today it is on the front line. so we have to look at the situation with different eyes than we did a few years ago. that is why i find it rather surprising - and so does the committee on the environment - that we are being asked to accept a number of additives for which we do not have all the information. of the nine commission proposals, we consider five to be suspect, for various reasons. i will briefly touch on them. first, an additive called sodium alginate is proposed for use on shredded carrots. the benefit to the consumer is that these shredded carrots will look "fresh" when they are not. it's quite clear, just read the literature on it. as for the risks to the consumer, they are apparently not very important, but the scientific committees which have studied the subject consider that the laxative effect of this additive may be combined with other problems of the same kind. so it's not necessarily advisable. since the aim is not to improve the situation for the consumer but, on the contrary, to mislead him, we believe that this additive should not be accepted. there is another additive that is a huge problem, and that is e 467. i will not give its official name because it is much too long and too complex, and i think that for translation it will be even more difficult. e 467 is a hazardous substance, containing extremely dangerous impurities. one of these impurities is ethylene oxide, which has been shown to be a carcinogen, and this is recognized by the scientific community. so we say no as well. finally, three other additives are proposed as technological auxiliaries. these are flammable gases: butane, isobutane and propane. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter, and whether it is not in the position to take a decision on the matter. so i briefly went over the problem. in our view, five additives should not be allowed, the other four being apparently not a problem when we read the scientific literature on them. we are not strict, so we feel that they can be accepted. i think it is important, mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, to give a new impetus to the current legislation on additives. the commission has announced a major revision of the directive. i think it would be a positive sign if we showed that we do not want to maintain the attitude that has been dominant for years and that has allowed additives to be more and more numerous, with combined effects, moreover, of which we do not know all. we think that we should instead go to a much smaller list and remove the most suspect additives from the list. i've personally picked up about 50 of them. others should be used much less widely. i am thinking, for example, of nitrites, nitrates and sulphites, which, as we know, pose well-established health problems.
- (nl) mr president, people's diet reflects their lifestyle. some people are rightly or wrongly critical of what i would call the modern western way of life. the answer to that question is often determined by the choices people make. the epp group believes that consumers should be able to make a choice, which implies not only information but also the guarantee that products on the market do not present any health risks and that all products are manufactured in accordance with the rules and procedures in force. the basic rules for additives are contained in the 1989 framework directive. this directive states that an additive may only be authorized if there is a sufficient technological need, if there is no danger to the health of the consumer and if the consumer is not misled. in our view, the latter condition is not fulfilled as regards the authorisation of sodium alginate for peeled and cut carrots. when a consumer buys fresh carrots, he must be able to consider that they have not been processed and that they have not undergone any treatment. he also expects that they have not been dipped in a preservative. the framework directive lists different objectives that are accepted in order to assess the need to use additives. one of these is the provision of dietary ingredients or components to patients on a diet. the use of e 467 proposed by the commission, and i am sticking to the abbreviation, seems to us to meet this objective. the framework directive also states that simplification of food production and preparation is an acceptable performance criterion. that is why our group and we are not as negative as the committee on the environment about the authorization of three additives in cooking sprays. all the other amendments of the committee on the environment are supported by us, as is the amendment just tabled by the pse group. if i may, i'd like to add a few things. firstly, the rapporteur - who has worked very hard and i commend him for this because i have rarely seen a rapporteur put so much effort into a report - is quite right to complain that the commission does not give parliament enough information in the com document. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he intends to address this problem in the future. secondly, the assessment of the public health implications must of course be carried out on a scientific basis. we have scientific advice from the scientific committee for food. after the bse crisis, the scientific committees were reformed at our request and, as we have said before, to our satisfaction. we now believe that we have two options: either we accept the opinions of the scientific committees in principle, or we do not accept them, but in that case the committees must be reformed again. but we can't eat at every grill. i am afraid that by not accepting the conclusions of the scientific committees in principle, the legislation we are drawing up and ourselves are not credible and that - which is difficult for me - we are substituting our conviction for the competence of the scientists who make up the scientific committees. thirdly, i would like to know whether the commission intends to regularly review the additives authorized in the light of the state of the art at the time, as the rapporteur rightly asks in his commentary. and finally, fourthly, a word on the hierarchy of standards. we are calling for a hierarchy of standards in the igc report and we have also discussed this elsewhere. i would like to know from the commissioner whether he thinks that drawing up or supplementing lists of additives is something that should be dealt with in the european parliament and the council, preferably by the codecision procedure, or whether this is a task for a controlled executive.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the recent dioxin and mad cow crises have amply demonstrated, if need be, that the consumer now wants to have all the guarantees on his food. in this respect, the lannoye report is a case study, one more. the rapporteur rightly asks whether additives in our food are really necessary and, more importantly, whether they are really harmless to our health. what are we talking about ? the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union does not use any of the 307 additives authorised by the union, of which only 163 are completely harmless, mr lannoye said. who benefits from the additives, this invasion of chemicals on our plates? not to the consumer, who is increasingly looking for 100% healthy food for himself and his children. in this connection, and without going into technical details, i would like to return to the three criteria highlighted in the report. firstly, do the additives meet a technological need? the least that can be said is that the consumer's interest does not always coincide with that of the producer firms. secondly, is the additive useful to the consumer? the rapporteur highlights at least one case where the commissioner could be misled: is it not a deception to affirm and prevent the legitimate bleaching of an aging, shredded carrot? asking the question is answering it. finally, is the safety of the additive ensured? once again, the precautionary principle must guide our thinking here, because choosing a carrot with e 401 is also, and the consumer does not know this, not always at all, exposing himself to potentially unpleasant side effects that cannot be ignored or minimized. in conclusion, the liberal group will support the lannoye report because it is very specifically aimed at improving the quality of life of consumers and at ensuring that they have the truth on their plates. this is the consideration that must take precedence over all others.
the rapporteur. - i swear, mr. president, i will be extremely brief. i do not think we have the time or the possibility of having a full debate on such a complex issue, but i would like to discuss it with the commissioner outside this house. first of all, i have a first comment to make on the scientific committees. it is not enough, commissioner, to read the conclusions of the scientific committees; you have to read the report, which i did. and sometimes, reading these reports, there is more doubt than certainty. i would therefore ask you to be a little more circumspect before rejecting all the amendments a priori, when i claim to have done scientific work as well. i have confronted a number of points of view, i have read the texts, and i can tell you that you are moving a little too fast in rejecting the amendments we are proposing. then there is another factor which has not been considered, namely the possible synergy between the multitude of additives found in food. we never study these additives together. we study them separately. and that's something you have to consider.

the rapporteur. - (nl) mr president, this debate on the guidelines on vertical restraints is the last in a series of four, which are tentatively on a highly technical subject. companies are all too aware of the importance of community competition policy to their actions, but let us not forget that good, fair and taxable competition rules also have consequences for the consumer. i have the impression that for people who are not familiar with competition policy, the issue of vertical restraints between companies seems a bit like a whirlwind quite outside their existence, but the situation is such that a large part of the daily distribution is carried out, managed and organised on the basis of the rules applicable to vertical restraints. i had intended, mr president, to explain this matter to my colleagues in more detail, but given the small number of people in the chamber, i do not think i should waste your time or that of the interpreters. the commissioner knows his record and the people who are going to speak on this matter know it at least as well as i do. i would just like to make the following points clear. as a parliament and as rapporteur on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, we can declare the validity of the commission's drawing up and publishing of guidelines. the commission is the european competition authority par excellence and everyone - and certainly legal certainty - will benefit from the commission's first very concrete explanation of its new policy. secondly, i hope that the commission will incorporate the remarks in the report of the economic committee, which i will not repeat here, into the final version of the guidelines which will be published shortly, and i hope to hear a statement to that effect, commissioner. then there are some questions. firstly, will we receive the required assessment after three or four years of the new approach to vertical restraints? secondly, when can we expect to receive proposals for a new approach to horizontal agreements, which approach has also been announced by the commission? thirdly, automotive distribution contracts are not covered by the new approach at the moment but the regulation on this sector expires in 2002. we can expect an evaluation from the commission this year. when will she come ? has the commission already consulted the motor vehicle distribution sector and, in particular, the small distributors? fourthly, we are still faced with a fundamental question, one which we have asked repeatedly and unsuccessfully and which i am therefore obliged to repeat once again: why has the commission decided to apply the new approach now in sectors where competition policy was running smoothly in its present form? in conclusion, mr president, i should like to thank the members who are unfortunately not here, and who have dealt with this subject in the economic committee, not only for this report but also for the three previous reports. i would like to inform those who tabled amendments - and these are mainly amendments tabled on behalf of the pse group - that i had to reject them for substantive reasons which i have already explained in the economic committee; the commission has already tabled these amendments and they have been rejected one after the other. mr president, i would like to apologize finally because i will not be able to be present at the vote tomorrow morning but mr von wogau will replace me as rapporteur if necessary.

problem in air transport is the peak noise during landings and take-offs, a problem which is of course exacerbated by its harmful consequences at night: night flights, or rather night take-offs and landings, make it impossible for some people living near airports to sleep. what are the responses to date on standards for aircraft? these were last established by the international civil aviation organization in 1977. nothing has therefore changed in this respect in the last 23 years and, as far as fleet renewal is concerned, it is clear that it is not at all stimulated by a change in standards in the direction of greater rigour, so that we are living with very old aircraft. the recent commission communication on air transport and the environment sets out a strategy for reducing noise based on both the use of economic instruments - better air traffic management and access to airports - and the promotion of technological progress. it is a good strategy, which will limit the increase in noise, but my feeling, and i believe that of many colleagues, is that it will not achieve, in the short term, the objective that no one should be exposed to noise levels that endanger health and the quality of life. in view of this situation, a number of countries and regions have adopted measures to restrict access to airports at night. unfortunately, this has been done in a certain disorder, which leads to a reaction, in my view, that is dangerous for certain transport companies, particularly those involved in the transport of mail and freight: they are raising the threat of relocation and are thus creating a certain economic insecurity in the regions concerned. i believe that the answer is to harmonize at european level: it is not a question of standardizing, of course, because there are regional differences, but of a common framework for the various member states of the european union. i also think that the organisation of night-time air transport activities, which are inevitably noisy regardless of the state of technology, is certainly something that needs to be regulated and limited. i therefore hope that the commission will take initiatives in this respect. i did not intervene in the debate earlier and, with your permission, i would like to say a few words on the vote. personally, i prefer a vote which results from the participation of many colleagues to a vote of 30 or 40 members on friday at noon. i am not happy about that, because a resolution voted by a small number of members has very little weight. i was involved in this morning's action and i did not like to be criticised by some colleagues who felt that it was a manoeuvre against strasbourg. i am not against strasbourg at all, quite the contrary. i have proved it many times, even though i am belgian. i do not want strasbourg to miss the sessions which normally run from monday to friday. - (nl) mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the aviation industry is a growing industry and certainly a flourishing industry in the european union. this situation has undoubtedly advantages, but it also has, increasingly, a very large number of negative aspects. i would like to say for a moment that if europe does not intervene in this problem, the quality of life, the economic and social aspects will compete with each other in an ever-growing way. the question is: are we able to ensure that our citizens have a night, or even a healthy day, of peace and quiet, and, above all, do we have the political will to do so? let me dwell for a moment on the problem of noise pollution at my own national airport, an airport which you all frequent very often, by the way. the noise pollution caused by zaventem has long since exceeded the acceptable threshold. the quality of life, the sleep of the people living near the airports is deplorable. the solution to this problem has been the subject of much publicity in belgium, including at the level of my government. you know that the liberals, the greens and the socialists are represented in this government. the minister for the environment had risked introducing a total ban on night flights. belgium suddenly became too small to support the violent discussion that broke out. commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, you can be a socialist and an environmentalist at heart, and i can assure you that i am. i also wish to continue to promote the life and quality of life of each individual as the supreme good. this is an admirable ideal in theory, but it seems sometimes very difficult to put into practice. which is worse ? sleeping very hard or very badly, night after night, or, overnight, losing your job and your income? the large number of companies operating in zaventem had threatened to eliminate hundreds, if not many more, of jobs. the unions and the employers suddenly got along. meanwhile, an alternative solution has been developed in belgium: a combination of measures including noise source limitation, strict rules to control compliance with these measures and a number of measures in the area of infrastructure such as insulation of houses. it is an improvement, not a solution. too many people suffer from too high a noise level in their bedroom. nevertheless, belgium and other member states are playing a pioneering role in this debate and the question is therefore: will the country and the businesses and people who work and live there be victims of these nuisances? we can only remedy this by tackling nighttime flying and noise pollution at european level. and that is what we are asking for, and i am very pleased to see that all the political groups in this parliament are asking for it, and i hope that the commission will not be deaf to our requests. the false dilemma between economic, social and ecological aspects will have to be solved by europe. we want a harmonised method of calculating noise pollution within the european union. we also want uniform rules and an efficient costing system. we also want criteria to be defined to determine when a ban on night flights within the union should be imposed. a recent commission communication on air transport and the environment sets out a first strategy. that's good, but it's not enough. firstly, as our colleague mr lannoye has already pointed out, it does not provide any solution to the problem at the moment. secondly, it is only a communication. we want real legislation from the commission so that everyone knows what to do with it. i regret that the commissioner, who is replacing commissioner de palacio, says that it is very difficult to introduce a ban on night flights. i think that this should be possible with a little goodwill. i am hopefully waiting for his answer.

the rapporteur. - (nl) mr president, madam commissioner, the question you are asking in your communication is really a question about the future of the air transport industry in europe. this is obviously the big question today, some ten years after the liberalisation of the sector began. this liberalization - as you yourself can see in your communication - has therefore entered a phase of development: new airlines have appeared, new routes have been created, new tariffs have in fact been introduced. this is a new kind of audience in the air transport industry. the existing national airlines, large and small, have become more profitable and have managed to operate more efficiently without the help of the public authorities. all these factors prove that liberalisation has allowed the market to evolve. we can say that in general liberalisation has brought benefits to passengers, but not to everyone. thus, for business class passengers, these advantages are much less important than for other passengers. the question is therefore: how strong is the basic structure of air transport in europe today? you point out in your communication that even today the explosion is more important here than in the united states. we have almost as many companies, but they only bring our share of the world market to 27%, compared to 38% for the americans, which is a huge difference. you also point out that the profitability of our companies is much lower than that of american companies. so we are asking questions about the viability of these new companies, because how long will they last and how solid are they really? these are questions that we need to ask ourselves. how many of these companies will still be active on the european market in about ten years' time? how sustainable and stable is the growth of this industry that we have seen over the last ten years? is our airspace overcrowded or poorly managed? our airports are cluttered. that is why it is very important, madam commissioner, that you make a final proposal on the management of airport slots. i think this is vital for airlines - particularly small ones - entering the market. we were asking questions about social development, about passenger rights. i would therefore like to ask you what the consequences of the agreement recently concluded with gibraltar on this matter will be. is there any connection with air travel? so these are the questions we are still asking ourselves about this. now that liberalisation has become a reality, further measures are needed. firstly, the europeanisation of air traffic control, of course. this is the problem dealt with in the report by our colleague mr atkins. i shall be tabling an oral amendment tomorrow on this subject, in order to replace the term privatisation with liberalisation. we have an agreement on this with the main groups. it is clear that europeanization must also be carried out at another level, that of security. a european security agency must be established quickly. of course, the member states must take a step backwards in both of these respects. this brings me to the crucial point of your communication, namely that there is no external policy on european air transport and that therefore duties on third countries are negotiated bilaterally. this is of course decisive for the whole structure of the air transport industry in europe. normally, after liberalisation, firms adapt their activities to the liberalised market. but in the airline industry, this is very rare. we have not seen any mergers. klm and alitalia tried one, but it failed, because of a number of complications related to nationality, if i may say so. the merger between swissair and sabena is still in place, but its future depends on an agreement with switzerland, which we also have to discuss this week, and on a referendum to be held in switzerland soon. it is noted that european companies do form alliances with american companies, but that their role in these alliances is less important than that of the american companies. that is why, as rapporteur, i would like to ask you a few questions about your proposal for a common transatlantic airspace. i think the principle is good, but since we do not yet have a solid structure for the european air transport industry, i wonder if this is going too fast. before taking this second step, a number of very complex problems must be solved. personally, as rapporteur, when it comes to transport, and therefore also air transport, i still have a certain suspicion of american nationalism. we must negotiate openly, with reciprocity - as they say - and european airlines must have exactly the same rights as american airlines. it can't be otherwise. only then will we be able to compete with the americans in a free market. an important question is what can we do without the americans. what are we going to do alone ? what is the viability of european industry if it is confined to european borders and these bilateral agreements? i do not say much about this in my report, but mrs lucas does in a report on the environment. the environment has its limits too, and this is a key question we must ask ourselves, perhaps not in the light of my report but in the light of mrs lucas' report. secondly, intermodality, which i also stress in my report. i think that the commission must also be clear on this and make proposals with the aim of promoting this intermodality. rail and air transport can work together very well in a number of areas. i would like to conclude, mr president, by saying that liberalization is a problem which has proved to be very important. i can see that too. but we're not even halfway there yet. the commission must quickly provide us with proposals, concrete action plans, and draft laws that we can debate in order to improve the situation on the ground. it is also clear that the member states have a very important responsibility and that much depends on them, in terms of the efforts they are prepared to make to ensure that europe has a proper air transport industry. so i think that parliament, together with the commission and the council, must act quickly, because the world will not wait for us. so we must get to work without further delay, or we will always be underpaid.
- (nl) mr president, i should like to begin by congratulating my colleague mr sterckx on his excellent report. i believe that this comes at a time when the air transport industry is making headlines with the takeover agreement between swiss air and sabena, the failure of the klm-alitalia marriage and the adventures around malpensa. the sector is therefore in a state of flux, which makes the report all the more timely. the report by mr sterckx shows that while there are many advantages to liberalization of air transport, it has not, or has not yet, brought us paradise on earth. just as europe and emu must be 'corrected' from an ecological and social point of view, so too will liberalisation have to be accompanied by measures to allow for better organisation of airspace, greater safety and more effective protection of consumers and the environment. the debate on night flights in strasbourg two weeks ago showed that there are many shortcomings in european air transport regulations. liberalisation without a common air transport policy and without a unified airspace can only lead to problems. it is estimated that air traffic will increase by a further 100% over the next 15 years. without strong action, we will not be left with one in three flights delayed and 450,000 flight hours lost each year. but what is stopping us from taking further action? it is a powerful airline lobby and a principle of sovereignty misunderstood by some countries which do not want to give up the management of their national airspace. we have a unified market. we know about the free movement of people. we have a common currency. so why can we not create a common european airspace more quickly? i also fully support the idea of creating a common transatlantic area. i hope that this will be done without any unilateral concessions on the part of europe. that is certainly the lesson we must learn from the adventures of these hushkits. i believe that we should take greater account of environmental criteria such as noise and emissions when allocating slots. the authorities must also invest more in measures to improve the quality of life of those living near airports. this is often the case, and this is where it is most useful, for small regional airports. in conclusion, i would like to make a plea for an aviation pax. a unified airspace, a common air transport policy, viable airports with a viable environment, improved security and strong european airlines are the ingredients of the aviation peace we must achieve. only when we have done so will the people of my city of ostend, at the noisy and dangerous airport, understand the full meaning of the word european union.

the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. - (nl) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, madam rapporteur, you have already delivered the resolution on ethiopia. well, it is a good illustration of the timeliness and necessity of food aid, even today. we cannot therefore refuse to adopt the revision of this food aid convention. but we will not adopt this revision just because international solidarity is essential to alleviate hunger, we will also adopt it because it contains a number of improvements compared to its previous versions. the nature and manner in which food aid has been provided under the convention has been criticised, and rightly so. this agreement is a framework for monetising surplus cereals from europe and the united states and selling them off our market. as a result, local food production is often disrupted and discouraged at the grassroots level. the convention is used as a "lubricant" for commercial transactions. moreover, it has often been at odds with social and cultural practices, in particular - as our rapporteur has already said - with the important role of women in the food supply. the transport and operating costs are enormous, and large sums of money are thus earned at the expense of the poor. the european union has rules of conduct which depart from these practices and it tries to enforce them in its food aid. these rules will now be incorporated into this international convention on food aid. we therefore believe that the convention has been greatly improved: it is no longer just cereals that are taken into account, but also other products such as local products, fish and vegetables, transport and operating costs must be proportionate to the value of these products and it is mainly the least developed countries that can call on aid. i think it is particularly important to promote local rural development. however, the new convention still presents a problem. european traders have managed to limit common aid in the form of loans to 20%. but the united states is keen on this form of aid, which continues to burden the most needy countries with debt, even if the interest rates applied are lower and the maturities are 30 years. but what is 30 years in the life of a people? we fully support the conventions which are intended to prevent many abuses, for example by separating aid from trade or by preventing the dismantling of local markets. this can be done very easily, both when buying the products and when selling the food aid on the local market, as speculation and price increases are quickly revealed. the example of ethiopia shows that not only the purchase but also the transport, distribution, storage etc. must be subject to strict controls. so, mr president, i ask myself the following question: we were in ethiopia, the orders were placed, everything was in stock, but there was no famine there, nor at that time. hence the question of whether we should refine our instruments.

- mr president, i fully support the rapporteur's view that piracy must be tackled with rigor and that we must take a comprehensive approach to the matter, that is to say both preventive and repressive. the economic consequences are indeed unpredictable. the damage is estimated to be between 5 and 7% of world trade. i therefore ask the commission whether this issue should no longer be included in the ongoing negotiations in the world trade organisation. the majority of piracy comes from outside the european union, whereas the damage is felt here. one example: if we were to reduce piracy by 10% in the computer sector alone, according to the latest studies, this would create 250 000 jobs in the european union. it is good to emphasize prevention. the european consumer must be made aware that buying counterfeit products is harmful in many ways, including to himself. it's holding back economic growth. it's a public health hazard, and it's a public safety hazard. worse still, more and more people are convinced that piracy is in the hands of organized crime, which is the source of revenue and money laundering. the action plan to combat organised crime of june 1997 already mentioned this and asked both the council and the commission to draw up common provisions to combat organised crime in the field of counterfeiting. this report is therefore not premature. it's a good report. the proper functioning of the internal market and free trade requires that creativity and innovation be stimulated and not undermined by abusive practices.
the commission has already submitted a proposal for a regulation to the council. - mr president, when i saw you in the chair i thought i would have to delete something in my text, but i am glad to see you in your position. i can assure mr laschet, ladies and gentlemen, on behalf of the committee on budgetary control, that we will give him our full support. i am very pleased that the committee on budgets, the committee on foreign affairs and the committee on budgetary control are on the same page this time. the proposal under discussion today is intended to provide a common legal basis for the financing of the united nations interim mission for kosovo and the office of the high representative for bosnia and herzegovina. as mr laschet has made very clear, the eu part of its funding has so far been within the framework of the common foreign and security policy. the sums involved are substantial: eur 28 million for the office of the high representative, of which the european union is still responsible for 53%, and eur 12 million for the united nations interim administration mission in kosovo (unmik). the advantage of this new arrangement is that the amounts are indeed deducted from the common foreign and security policy pillar. what did we establish in the past? we noted that the european commission, which is responsible for the implementation of the budget under the treaties, was not in control of events under the current arrangements, and the committee on budgetary control has already pointed this out on several occasions. in practice, this meant that the commission was merely playing the role of treasurer, of accountant, and that ultimately led to lax policies, lax behaviour, a lack of commitment. the consequences of this are shown in a recent special report by the court of auditors. for example, we know that the financial controller in bosnia and herzegovina invoiced his services to the office of the high representative under a contract with an undertaking of which he was the manager. i thought that was called a conflict of interest. the committee on budgetary control will come back to this special report later. in any event, the draft regulation before us will give parliament greater control over the financing of these peace agreements and will enable us, for example, to put funds in reserve if we want to enforce better management of the funds. the responsibility for implementing the funds is no longer shared between the commission and the council but is now vested in the commission. i think that is a good thing, because it means that we can also use our discharge procedure as a means of control in the future. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i think we should give our full support to mr laschet's report unanimously.

even if it is only a simple compact disc, can serve as an example and have an educational value, especially for young people: they must learn that a copy hurts the artist they love. as regards the law enforcement aspect, we must commit our member states to a more voluntary policy of swift and effective prosecution of offenders. i also think it would be useful if the law enforcement agencies were of a healthy economy. but above all, they mislead the consumer, sometimes at the expense of his safety or health. this consumer must be protected, that is obvious. it is in this spirit that i support the rapporteur. - (nl) mr president, i should first like to thank the president, mr provan, for his friendly words, but above all for the way in which he has continued and guided the whole process with the collaboration of mr rocard. i would also like to thank all my colleagues, because the search for a compromise on such delicate and difficult points can only be done if we all put a little water in our wine, and that is what has happened. thank you for that. with the vote in the european parliament tomorrow and in the council the day after tomorrow, a decade of european legislation on several aspects of the organisation of working time is thus coming to a provisional end. i say 'provisionally' because we have developed exclusively horizontal regulation for a number of sectors and activities, while we are still waiting for vertical rules. these should be developed within the framework of the cta, but in the absence of a cta the commission itself must take the initiative and submit proposals for directives. this has already been done in the case of road transport. the european parliament has already spoken on this subject, but the council does not seem to be in a position to adopt a common position. this is unacceptable ! this is really unacceptable because this is one of the main sectors, a sector which is experiencing enormous difficulties and is hit by a multitude of accidents due to the lack of regulation and lack of control in the member states. i urge the council and the social partners to find a solution to these problems once and for all. as far as inland waterway transport is concerned, no proposal for a directive has yet been tabled; i therefore call on the commission to get to work. fortunately, air and sea transport, as well as rail transport, have since concluded agreements which have become directives. as for the sea fishing and the offshore sector, as well as the activities of doctors in training, they are covered, like rail transport, by the directive we are discussing today. in the case of sea fishing, the european parliament has succeeded in extending the maximum working week to 48 hours over a reference period of 12 months. derogations are possible only in the framework of a cta or after consultation with the social partners. it is also necessary to regulate this situation, because fishermen not only spend long periods at sea - 3000 hours a year on average in some countries - but they also do very hard work, and this in extremely mobile circumstances. as regards the doctors in training, i refer to the speech by mr provan, who explained very well what we have finally achieved. the most important point is that countries which want a transition period of more than nine years have to go through a very difficult procedure to get it. the reason for their request and the final decision must be published in the official journal of the european union. i hope that this procedure will have a deterrent effect on these countries, because we can accept a transitional period of more than nine years with the number of working hours provided for. when i signed the directive on various aspects of the organisation of working time in 1993 as president of the council, i was aware that the commission's 1990 proposal did not exclude any sector or activity. but a directive applicable to all workers and to all sectors and activities does not seem realistic to the council. the commission therefore proposed a separate and complementary initiative for the sectors and activities excluded by the council. it therefore published the 1997 white paper and the 1998 consultation paper and communication. this directive is the first to be drawn up within the framework of european social policy following negotiations within a conciliation committee. the european parliament acted as a co-legislator. the co-decision procedure played a decisive role in this. i think that this success, the fact that we have finally reached a good compromise, is due in part to the wisdom of parliament, which has tabled only a few amendments. because if we end up with a conciliation procedure and parliament feels that it has to table 30 or 40 amendments in this procedure, it is particularly difficult, if not impossible, to reach a compromise. we managed to avoid it. the european parliament has thus demonstrated a legislative maturity which the council and especially the intergovernmental conference should not lose sight of.
- (nl) mr president, commissioner, all this is happening to me at air traffic control. it is high time that the member states abandon their attitude of national particularism, because it is this behaviour that has prevented rail from becoming important and retaining its customers for freight and passenger transport. the result is the opposite. the excellent savary report is the first in a long series of reports which we shall have to read on the liberalization of the railway sector. i think it's very important. there must be no frontiers for trains within the european market. i think it's important. in a large market like europe, trains should not know borders. i think this is the best solution for both the customers and the service. if the best solution had been cooperation between national companies, they could have implemented it long ago and we would not have had the disaster of the railway recession. for a country like mine, and there are other colleagues who have spoken about their country, it is obviously very important that we achieve a european market characterized by technical uniformity, that is to say, interoperability as it has been so nicely named. a small country is certainly very sensitive to this, especially if it is located at important crossroads. i think we need to act quickly and in the most radical way possible. one critical point concerns the staff. i am glad, mr rapporteur, that a compromise has been reached in this respect. it is of course important that agreements can be reached on health and safety, without compromising on these aspects, and it goes without saying that we must also be very clear on training and harmonize all these aspects. but i have to ask myself: are we ambitious enough ? commission. - (es) mr president, i should like to thank mr goebbels for his report and the various participants in this debate. today we are analysing the report on convergence, which deals with the swedish and greek problems. i respect sweden, the situation has hardly changed since 1998. the economic situation is positive, but the central bank's legislative adjustment is not effective and it is not part of the exchange rate mechanism of the european monetary system. the situation in greece is radically different. greece did not meet the convergence criteria in 1998 but does now. by that i mean that it is strictly addressing it, with exactly the same interpretation as in 1998. of course, greece has made a great effort and i have nothing to add to the positive results mentioned by mr goebbels in his report. i would like to elaborate on some of the points that have been made today because the greek model raises some concerns insofar as greece is a precedent for the new candidates for membership but also for the current members of the union who, in the future, would become members of the single currency. in this sense, it is absolutely clear that our attitude towards greece will be fundamental, but i would stress that we have acted towards greece in the same way as we have towards other countries when we analysed their situation in 1998. there are basically two areas of concern: debt and inflation. are they sustainable? is this inflationary situation sustainable - or lasting as you say ? it is true that in the case of greece the debt exceeds 100% of gdp; a situation which is virtually equivalent to that of belgium and italy. it does not seem reasonable to me to compare the present situation of greece with the present situation of belgium and italy. it should be borne in mind that belgium and italy benefited from debt reduction through lower interest rates, mainly due to the convergence of their central banks' interest rates with those of the european central bank. in terms of comparison, the greek situation is similar to the italian or belgian situation at the time. the second area of concern is inflation. the committee considers that this concern is based on two arguments: firstly, it is true that the level of inflation needed to meet the criteria has been reached relatively recently; secondly, it is also true that there will be a fall in interest rates which will undoubtedly have a positive effect and will encourage an acceleration in the reduction of the deficit and debt, but which may generate market tensions. as regards the first point - has greece reached this situation of unsustainably low inflation? - i have to make a few comments. greece has already been meeting the inflation criterion for a few months and the situation is improving significantly. this morning i had the opportunity to speak about the greek figures published a few days ago by the greek national statistical office. this morning, the figures from eurostat were also published. the harmonised cpi for greece is currently 2.1%, down by seven tenths compared with march figures; the situation is better than in some countries of the monetary union, such as belgium, spain, ireland, italy, luxembourg and finland. therefore, the situation of greece, in terms of comparison, is today frankly good. if we stick to the strict criterion of the last 12 months, we find that inflation is currently at 2%. the situation is clearly better than that of spain and ireland, and virtually identical to that of italy, luxembourg or the netherlands. so in terms of figures, i think we should not be worried about the greek situation. but the commission also shares the concern expressed by some members. that is why we emphasized two points when we discussed the broad guidelines of economic policy and the convergence report. greece must do two things - and the greek government is willing to do so and has committed itself to doing so - first, to pursue its policies of liberalization and structural reforms to improve the inflation situation; second, greece should continue its deficit reduction policy, but in a policy mix which would allow it, in the event that higher demand due to lower interest rates would generate some inflationary pressures, to reconsider its fiscal policy so that it does not undermine the process of integration into monetary union. i'm convinced that all of this is going to happen. this is the commitment of the greek government. i therefore believe that greece's entry into the euro will not be an obstacle to the euro but a support. the euro is proving to be an attractive currency for some countries to join. i hope that the path traced by greece today can be followed by other member states in the coming years. it is the end of a process. we have to wait for the june decisions to be taken, but i am convinced that by welcoming greece to the euro when the time comes, we will inspire more confidence than anything else in the future development of the single currency.
the commission always respects the treaty; it is obliged to apply it. ladies and gentlemen, remember that it is true that sweden is not participating because its central bank has not adapted to european conditions, a decision which is a matter for swedish legislation. secondly, it should also be borne in mind that participation in the exchange rate mechanism of the european monetary system is a voluntary decision. you may ask whether this is not contrary to the spirit of the maastricht treaty, but this is a point which we cannot logically discuss now.

role, i want to say a few words about the evaluation itself. commissioner, it is particularly difficult to make an assessment when there is no data to verify precisely how far the various european countries have progressed in recent years. there's no encrypted data. there are national reports on the 12 subjects we have to discuss in new york, reports which show precisely where the evaluation is. i am therefore glad that you mentioned, during the discussion on the fifth action programme, that you wanted to set up a kind of observatory which would try to collect all the data broken down by gender. because there is a huge gap in this matter. a second point and i am addressing parliament itself. the way we have had to work to produce this report indicates that the secretariat of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities is sorely short of staff. the collaboration was very difficult to obtain and if mrs gröner and i had not had good collaborators, there would probably never have been a report. i find it scandalous for parliament that other committees can apparently have a very good secretariat and a well-equipped secretariat with a large staff, but that this is not possible for the committee on women's rights. it may be helpful for women to take the initiative in this regard. that's it for the evaluation. i won't dwell on that. i think that the action programme, the five-year programme which the commission is now putting forward for discussion, is of great importance for the next five years. it takes up the classic themes, of course, and that's normal because the world is progressing, but these different themes are still on the agenda, with new accents it is true, but in the end, it is always these themes that come back. the theme of the world of work. i think the new emphasis should be on new technologies and the absence or lack of women in this sector. that was said earlier too. the division between work and family, as provided for in the employment guidelines. equal work, equal pay. this is something which i think the commission is not doing enough. i think that we are paying very little attention to this. the gender pay gap remains. the decision-making process. the commission has made efforts. europe has made efforts, especially in the political field. i do not think that is the case with the social partners. they need much more attention, since they manage and control a large part of social life. the violence. i believe that too little attention is paid to domestic violence. the focus is on workplace violence and so on, but not so much on domestic violence. the media. i think they deserve more attention too. they are so crucial to social life. women are very few in number. then, as regards women's rights and human rights, i hope that progress can be made at the intergovernmental conference in this area too. finally, i have already said that europe is expected to be a pioneer. i am thinking here of two specific areas. firstly, when we give development aid or have contacts with third countries, let us not only impose conditions on the economic position of women. we know her. we know that in many developing countries, women are the drivers of the economy, and we support that. we know that now. what we do not emphasize enough in our contacts with third countries is that human rights must be respected also in relation to women. what's happening in afghanistan is beyond description. it is unacceptable that circumcision continues to be practised in a number of countries and that we maintain, as if it were a matter of course, the best relations with these countries. then, a second point. the commission's screening report on the enlargement of the former eastern bloc will be essential to ensure that the situation in these countries is monitored very closely, since it shows a decline rather than an increase.
the rapporteur. - (nl) mr president, commissioner diamantopoulou, ladies and gentlemen, i should like first of all to thank all those who have collaborated on this report, that is to say all the people, all the staff but also all the colleagues of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities and the interpreters, because their contribution has also proved to be important. this report is also a response to the excellent commission communication on new measures to combat trafficking in women. in preparing the report, the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities held a public hearing with experts, in the presence of a victim who came to testify. a delegation from the commission, accompanied by commissioner diamantopoulou, visited a reception centre for victims of human trafficking. a survey was also sent to the ministries of foreign affairs of the member states and the candidate countries to learn more about their approach to trafficking in women. the results of this survey will be forwarded to the european commission. the report focused primarily on the fight against trafficking in women, but also on the fight against trafficking in children and men, in short, on the fight against trafficking in human beings in general. children are trafficked and used like adults. men too are sold into human trafficking and sexually abused. the sex industry is not the only market for these victims. in belgium, 40 per cent of them find themselves on the labour market, in sectors such as textiles, catering, fruit picking, baking and construction. some of them also work as domestic servants, as au pairs, but always in shameful conditions worthy of slavery. these victims are also recruited in the marriage market and in sports and football clubs. the united nations and the international organisation for migration (iom) estimate that four million people are trafficked worldwide each year, and 500,000 of them enter europe. there is every indication that the number of victims is increasing and that the flows from central and eastern europe have increased dramatically, exceeding those from africa, latin america, the caribbean and asia. in one of its reports, the united nations emphasizes that human trafficking is one of the fastest growing forms of organized crime. the huge revenues and relative freedom that this form of crime affords criminals are certainly not new to them. the penalties for drug traffickers, for example, are much more severe than those for trafficking in women. the illegal situation in which the victims find themselves often prevents them from speaking out. since there is clear evidence that these problems are developing in the sphere of international networks, it is important that they be addressed and resolved at european level. if we are to combat trafficking in human beings in an appropriate way, we must first define it clearly. secondly, priority must be given to the fight against organised networks which are not only involved in human trafficking but also in all kinds of other criminal activities such as drug trafficking, arms trafficking and other activities. that is why i welcome the new community initiatives on money laundering, an activity in which the proceeds of trafficking in women also play an important role. various initiatives have already been taken at european level. the awareness-raising started off well, with activities by interpol, europol, initiatives in each member state, habitat projects, etc. but europe must give priority to harmonizing the various national laws in this area and the resulting methods of detection and prosecution. an investigation carried out in the context of this report has clearly shown that there are important differences in this area between the member states and the applicant countries. some countries with strict laws on prostitution have no laws on human trafficking at all. with the introduction of the stop programme, europe has been able to react energetically to current events and has opened up possibilities for collaboration. last year the council adopted the daphne programme, a new policy instrument for the prevention of all forms of violence - physical, sexual and moral - against women and children. it is encouraging to note that the commission intends to present, in the first half of this year, detailed proposals for the harmonisation of criminal provisions in the member states concerning trafficking in human beings for the purpose of sexual exploitation, in particular as regards the granting of temporary residence permits to victims who are willing to cooperate in the investigation necessary for the prosecution of traffickers. looking at the amendments, i have the impression that some people want to use this report, which deals with the victims of human trafficking, to force a discussion on prostitution. the members of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities have expressly chosen to use this report as a framework for discussion on trafficking in women, on forced prostitution. the debate on the prohibition or legalization of prostitution is certainly necessary, but it cannot take place here, and this is in the interests of the victims of human trafficking. as i said, human trafficking is not just a prostitution problem. in addition, the victims of trafficking in women have great difficulty in reintegrating into their home countries and even their families. if they have worked in prostitution, they bear the stigma of it for the rest of their lives. you've been a bitch, you'll be a bitch. the initiatives taken so far are a first step towards a solution, but the number of victims has been steadily increasing. so we have some work to do. we have put forward proposals in the areas of prevention, reception of victims and legislation. a society faced with this kind of phenomenon has a moral debt to its victims and must give priority to the fight against human trafficking and to helping victims.
in writing. - (nl) i fully support the proposal to fill the 35 vacancies in the 'enlargement' reserve in the translation sector, provided that all the languages of the applicant countries are represented. that is why i voted in favour of amendment no 2. it is essential that the ep prepares effectively for future enlargement to central and eastern europe. this will require, in particular, the timely training of first-rate translators and interpreters. this has not always been the case in previous enlargements. there are three reasons why multilingualism is an essential condition for the proper functioning of the ep. the first is the principle of equality. all members must be able to carry out their work under the same conditions. the second is the democratic legitimacy of the ep. the latter is the democratic representation of the people. the third is the usefulness of multilingualism. it increases the efficiency of parliament's work. the debate is urgently needed if we are to give concrete form to all these aspects. in this connection, i refer you to the final report of the working party on multilingualism chaired by mr cot, which suggests a number of practical options. in short, it is clear that the linguistic preparation for enlargement is no longer postponed.
in writing. - (nl) i should like to congratulate my colleague and party comrade mr ferber on his report, although i do not agree with all the points and i did not respect his directive to vote for amendment no 2 and against amendment no 8 to his motion for a resolution. i voted in this way with the utmost conviction, because i believe that it is not by supporting the proposal of the secretary-general (and by providing 35 vacancies in the translation services), but by not supporting it, that the european parliament will send a political signal or take the lead in a political decision. as long as the rules of this house on the use of languages are not changed, the applicant countries and ourselves can assume that the language regime remains as it is; this means, firstly, that each member of parliament can speak in his own language at official meetings. secondly, that each member of parliament should have documents in his own language. and thirdly, that we treat not only the members of this house but above all the citizens whom we represent equally.
in writing. - (nl) i voted for the goebbels report and therefore for greece's membership of the euro zone. i voted in favour of the amendments to the pomés-ruiz report with the same conviction. they draw our attention to a sensitive issue which provides a great deal of material for discussion not only in the debate in plenary but also in the meetings of the economic and monetary affairs committee. many colleagues have doubts about the sustainability of the member states' convergence and stability policy. we have all seen that the pleas to greece to continue its efforts to reduce its debt and to achieve lasting stability are not only linked to the situation in greece itself but also to the dissatisfaction with the way other member states are behaving despite very high debt levels and not making enough use of the increased possibilities for debt reduction offered to them by economic growth and low interest rates. i hope that this obscure criticism of parliament will reach the ears of those concerned, and i am thinking in particular of the belgian government, which has already made so many promises that even the press is beginning to wonder about the consequences of this drift in expenditure. it is precisely because the hesitations expressed about greek membership do not concern greece so much, or because they lead to general criticism, that i believe greece should not be a victim. greece is welcome in the euro area and all member states must respect the stability and growth pact.

task will not be easy. the majority of my colleagues know very well why this directive is important. by wanting the treaty's principle of non-discrimination to be implemented swiftly and vigorously, europe wants to send a clear signal to the citizens of our own countries and those of the applicant countries. europe wants to show that it is serious about the fight for equal opportunities, regardless of race or ethnic origin, because we know that discrimination still exists in our countries, so that racism and extreme forms of nationalism still exist - more pronounced than in the past - and because racist parties exploit the idea of 'our people first'. that is precisely why it is important that the european parliament should strengthen, and even support, the commission's position, because we will soon need a valid and effective directive which combats all forms of discrimination based on race and ethnic origin. in conclusion, i would like to mention a few of the priorities identified by my group in terms of content. firstly, it is very important that the non-discrimination directive be broad and effective. that is why we want to extend the scope of the directive to groups of people who are victims of discrimination, as well as to areas such as housing, education, the operation of public services, the behaviour of the police. we are also pleased with the solution we have finally found to discrimination on the grounds of religion, which cannot give rise to a form of disguised ethnic discrimination. secondly, the strength of this directive depends entirely, in our view, on its binding nature, on the control of discrimination, on the effectiveness of the measures taken against discrimination; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented. mr oostlander, with all due respect and sympathy, let me point out that your 'clean-up operation' would have the very effect of depriving the directive of one of the pillars of strengthening the fight against discrimination. i say no, this is going too far. my group also supports the strengthening and support of the independent monitoring bodies. i am sure that in my country this directive will enable the centre for equal opportunities to strengthen its action. in conclusion, i would like to share one last thought with you on the sharing of the burden of proof. i am not aware of the reluctance that this arouses in some people. everyone should understand that it is impossible for an immigrant to prove that he has been discriminated against in employment or in his relations with his employers unless the accused is also asked to at least prove that the facts alleged by the complainant are unfounded. we women know this very well. that is why we have fought for so long for a directive on the sharing of evidence, and all we want, the men and women of the socialist group, is that immigrants should be treated in the same way as women. we will therefore also support the commission's proposal and will not allow any amendments to be made to it.

- (nl) mr president, i wish to follow with enthusiasm the path traced by our rapporteur, mr maaten, and i ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to do the same. of course, tobacco is not a product like any other. we know the risks of death associated with it, we know the statistics - our colleagues have already mentioned them. we also know the addictive effect of tobacco. regularly smoked cigarettes are especially dangerous, life-threatening. if you drive, you can have an accident, you can drink too much, but if you smoke, there is only one possibility: you are putting your life at risk. i am glad to see that my colleague mr maaten has stressed the dangers of passive smoking. a ban on smoking would not be effective or socially realistic at this time. but it is clear that those who are currently marketing cigarettes should not be allowed to do so. this product is far too dangerous. what can we do as legislators? first of all, we must realize the utter absurdity of the situation, for while tobacco generates a large amount of revenue for the state, it also generates a huge amount of expenditure for health insurance. we must dare to say it clearly. the least that can be said is that european tobacco policy is not very logical. as legislators, we must at least try to limit the harm of tobacco by obliging producers to warn the consumer in the clearest possible way. it may be bureaucratic, but in view of the fate of producers of other bonds, i think we must be very clear in our legislation. parliament will be discussing phthalates and plasticizers for pvc later this week, and when i see the caution and vigilance with which we in this parliament treat these products, and when i compare it with the little we ask for a product as dangerous as tobacco, i see that this parliament can be very selective in its indignation.

the rapporteur. - (nl) madam president, the entry into force of the internal market and the abolition of border controls have led to the disappearance of vat declarations and statistics, which were the responsibility of the customs services. intrastat, the statistical information system on the movement of goods, is not highly regarded by businesses, for whom the administrative burden is all the greater because, except in the case of information on the products concerned, precise data must be provided which are not the responsibility of the businesses directly concerned by the transaction but of the transport companies they use. the basic regulation stipulates that for statistics used for the classification of goods, the persons required to provide information must use the combined nomenclature, which comprises more than 10 500 subdivisions. the combined nomenclature is a tariff and statistical nomenclature used in trade between member states and with third countries. in the context of the slim initiative, the simplification of intrastat is designated as a pilot project. the slim-intrastat working party has proposed to simplify this classification and to adapt it more closely to the real needs of users without affecting its link with the classification used for trade in goods with third countries and its consistency with other statistical classifications. this proposal has also received support from representatives of professional associations and companies, as they themselves contributed to the development of the recommendations. the simplifications must in the first place benefit small and mediumsized enterprises. the number of codes is simplified, as is the classification and codification of goods by firms, while avoiding the need for firms to take special measures. this proposal and all the other measures to simplify the system can only have a beneficial effect on businesses. the european community should be able to take the initiative in proposing, at international level, to revise the structure of the harmonised system and to adapt it better to the needs of international trade in the 21st century. finally, i should like to point out that a tripartite consultation at the three meetings of the european parliament delegation finally led to a compromise at the meeting of the conciliation committee. the report was unanimously adopted without amendments in the committee on legal affairs and the internal market. i would therefore be grateful if my colleagues could support this report unanimously tomorrow.

in writing. - tobacco kills: 500,000 deaths a year in the eu, 1,400 a day, almost one a minute. one in two smokers will die sooner or later from a smoking-related disease. these numbers speak for
