- mr president, 'healthy and controlled from fork to fork' is now the formula that sums up the policy of consumer protection and the food chain that the european union wants. it was a pressing necessity. the dioxin crisis that struck belgium more than a year ago has left its mark on the minds of people all over europe. this crisis has not only cost billions of euros, but has also raised even more suspicion about the contents of our plates. more than ever, the citizen is calling us to account and demanding strict standards for healthy eating. this directive is the first in a long series of decisions on food safety. what can i say in a minute ? very quickly we must insist on amendment no 37 on dioxin. the standards we set for ourselves are indeed very, very high. but until we have more scientific analysis, we must not take any chances. pcbs and dioxins are toxic substances, even in very low doses. our rapporteur has said what we should think of the dilution principle, which would be a terrible signal, in my view, to send to consumers and producers. more than ever, this consumer needs reassurance. this directive helps to ensure that, as i said, we have a healthy and quality food from fork to fork. our citizens are demanding it.
- mr president, i would like to join all those who have congratulated the rapporteur, mrs paulsen, both on the quality of her report and on her openness to the various amendments which have been tabled. i should like to remind you at the outset that when we talk about unwanted substances and products, we immediately think of dioxins and pcbs, but there are also heavy metals and, more generally, persistent organic pollutants, most of which are now known to be hormone disruptors, that is to say substances which already produce effects in infinitesimal doses. it is therefore a question of adopting limit values which are dictated by reality, since our environment is polluted by these substances, but which must also be as low as possible to limit the risks. it is rather strange that the commission proposal does not propose limit values for pcbs and that it proposes limit values for dioxins only in the case of citrus pulp, which is still rather bizarre. in reality, this is the result of a disastrous event that occurred two years ago: citrus pulp from brazil was found to have high levels of dioxin due to the drying process used. there are inconsistencies with regard to heavy metals. for example, for fish meal the limit value is different from that proposed for products of non-marine animal origin. all of this was the subject of amendments by my group which were largely adopted by the committee on the environment - i thank it for that - despite the negative vote of the epp members. i hope they will change their minds before the plenary session, because i do not see what arguments justify the inconsistencies in the commission's draft. it seems to me that, given the mood in this house, we can approach the vote with confidence and hope to obtain a majority which will enable us to put our point of view to the vote at second reading.

- mr president, the european defence industry is important, but at least as important is world peace. mr titley's report offers a balanced answer to this recurring dilemma. it calls for better and more comprehensive controls on the arms trade and greater transparency. for example, it is necessary to develop better techniques for marking and tracing weapons, similar to the numbering of cars. this must be done in a harmonised way and at international level. it is the only way to make it easier to detect and punish obscure and illegal practices, because - and this seems to me to be the main problem - the distinction between legal and illegal arms trade is becoming ever smaller. the globalisation of world trade makes arms flows cheaper, faster and harder to trace. oxfam estimates that fifty-five percent of the total trade in small arms is illegal. a stricter code of conduct is of course only appropriate if the member states also assume their responsibilities. gun controls in certain air and sea ports leave much to be desired. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the acquis in the field of the environment. they do subscribe to the code but in certain cases they act as a hub for the illegal arms trade. some will suggest that the code of conduct is too unambitious, but better an imperfect code of conduct than none at all. in any event, the next annual reports should contain more complete and transparent information and the code of conduct should eventually also become legally binding. this code of conduct, mr president, is a first step towards greater consistency, but i hope, together with the rapporteur, that it will not stop there.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it was a sunday evening two weeks ago in the south of france that johan, 17, and robert, 16, killed their childhood friend romain with a gun and a baseball bat, for no reason, without motive, as if on television. they were quickly arrested and told the police: "it's funny, in soap operas you don't get caught that easily". they also said they wanted to see what it was like to actually kill someone. it's not far and it's not long ago. it happened on september 17th, near our home in europe. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. a few years ago, as you may recall, oliver stone's film born killers created a similar controversy by making young emulators in the united states and france. so when i hear some people in committee argue that there is no evidence of the harmful influence that violence in all its forms can have on children, i am surprised and i am using a euphemism. we are often frightened by the television programs our children watch. violence of all kinds, whether on television or on the computer, has short, medium and long-term effects. the aim of this report is to help parents, grandparents like us, to protect minors, to provide them with a tool that allows them to make a better informed choice for their children. so where in the world is subsidiarity being attacked here? the audiovisual sector is and remains the exclusive competence of the member states. the report does not mention any possible harmonisation. at most, there is talk of a timid convergence. the definition of common criteria for the protection of minors in the media is therefore necessarily minimal, given the cultural, moral and social variations in our member states. minimum standards are the least the union can do for its children. i also want to support the rapporteur in saying that we must ensure that all families have the right to have an affordable filtering system. however, these measures should not, and cannot, absolve broadcasters and educators of their responsibilities. children will be handling these filtering systems better than we are, very quickly. the legislator - i conclude here, mr president - cannot regulate everything, but he must sometimes remind everyone of their rights and duties. i think that's what we're doing today.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, although the text is not perfect, as the chairman of the committee on legal affairs and the rapporteur have already pointed out, i dare to call this legislative resolution an example of transparent, simplified and efficient community legislation. the community customs code has succeeded in harmonizing and simplifying legislation in a transparent manner, which is what we should always be aiming for. the customs code can only be welcomed, and i am therefore somewhat more positive than mrs ferrer, for example, because it is indeed the basis for various community policies in the context of joint action by the national customs administrations. the objective of achieving simplified but high-quality communication, with sufficient flexibility in the basic regulation, with improved collection procedures and with greater alignment of customs provisions, is one of the requirements of the single market and has been achieved here, so much so that many countries have been inspired by this to participate quickly in international trade. more generally, the community customs code is one of the most important legal instruments for determining the amount of duties due and for determining who is liable for those duties. the code is the legal basis for the levying of these duties and is therefore one of the main instruments for the protection of the community's financial interests and for the proper functioning of community policies in various fields. in short, i think it is fair to say that the administration now has all the levers at its disposal to carry out its task efficiently and in a limited time, and i assume that the european parliament may invite the administration to make the results of this legislative resolution known on the ground as soon as possible.

- the president, ladies and gentlemen, madam commissioner, first of all i would like to thank all the rapporteurs. i believe they have worked hard. it is a triumph every time to bring the budget round to a successful conclusion and i think that a congratulatory note is in order. the budget, ladies and gentlemen, is always more than a cold dance of numbers. behind every figure, behind every budget line, behind every sum allocated, there is a political policy to be pursued, there are political objectives, there are immense expectations, and above all there are people. i have experienced this firsthand during a tour of the western balkans in the last two weeks, visiting kosovo and bosnia. and i was in pristina when kostunica was celebrated in biarritz and promptly and unconditionally received 200 million euros in winter aid. a political act, ladies and gentlemen, a political act, commissioner, which was not exactly welcomed by the albanian kosovars. why, i was asked, is the union not using the change of power in serbia to put continuous pressure on the new regime, if only to secure the immediate release of the 914 people held in serbian prisons? - the president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, der mensch ist, was er is, man is what he eats. that, ladies and gentlemen, was indeed the first word i said in my maiden speech last year on the belgian dioxin crisis. it is also the words with which my colleague mr bowis here begins his explanatory statement and speech. it is clear that mr bowis and i, together with the whole environment committee, are largely on the same page, and i thank mr bowis for the very constructive cooperation he has shown over the past few months. i would like to make four points. the report rightly distinguishes between risk analysis and risk management. in this context, i would first of all like to say that ensuring food safety depends on the government's organising strong controls, and those controls are only as strong as the weakest link. if the weakest link breaks, the controls will fall like a house of cards. this means that all control services must work together well and often too little attention is paid to those control services which are responsible for checking the veterinary medicinal products; in my country, that's the pharmaceutical inspectorate. i therefore regret that in my country this service is not or hardly involved in the future operation of the federal food safety agency. experience shows that poor control of veterinary medicines and poor control of veterinary prescribing practices can lead to scandals. so pay attention to that. secondly, the bowis report makes it clear that the european food agency is responsible for risk analysis. we must be well aware that this agency will only be a strong partner in the food safety debate if it gains respectability over the coming years. that respectability will come, not through laws, not through regulations, but through integrity, through academic excellence, through independence, through accountability, through transparency and through comprehensibility. i therefore advocate that when scientific opinions are weighed up, minority views should always be published. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the hormones. i am also pleased, ladies and gentlemen, with the farmer-to-table and the rich-to-fork approach. i would even go further and advocate a semen-to-spoon approach. that means that food safety checks are carried out on all products, and i refer to paragraph 11 of the resolution. fourthly, parliament rightly stresses in paragraph 17 that the rapid alert system must be extended to all aspects of food safety, including animal feed, and we therefore rightly call for the rapid introduction of a positive list, commissioner. colleagues, this is just the beginning, we have a long way to go. we must realize that consumer confidence in food must be restored, and that is a fundamental human right. i hope that in the months and years to come we will be following the commission very closely in the implementation of the white paper.

- mr president, the fact that the white paper on food safety itself has no legislative significance does not in any way diminish its undoubted double value. firstly, it contains a commitment to a comprehensive and thorough renewal of legislation, to the establishment of an independent food safety authority and to better organised control. it also has a symbolic meaning. it can and should be the beginning of a new beginning. the policy will now be coordinated and integrated and cover the entire food chain. it is good that the rapporteur, mr bowis, who has done an excellent job, is taking a positive approach to the white paper. the report does not propose amendments but emphasizes them. it is rightly emphasized that the food authority must focus primarily on food safety. i hope, however, that there will also be room for scientific research on the relationship between diet and health. the policy must not only aim at preventing harm to health but also at achieving health benefits. a second, and rightly so, emphasis is on the need to take account of regional and cultural differences in the food supply and the role of small and mediumsized enterprises and the craft sector in this respect. let this be a call not to ignore the specific nature of small and mediumsized enterprises and craft industries in the vast legislative package that awaits us. the third point is missing from the report and we can add it by means of amendment no 12. both the white paper and the bowis report speak of communication on the one hand and of emergency procedures on the other, but there is no mention of the urgent need for internal and external crisis communication. however, and we can speak of this in belgium, it can be decisive for controlling the scale of the crisis and for the rise or fall of consumer confidence in the institutions. and to conclude, the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

commission. – i am delighted to be able to comment on this report and to commend the excellent work done by my honourable friend, john cushnahan. he and i are old friends and, more importantly, old friends of hong kong. both of us speak with – what i could describe with an understatement as – a degree of knowledge of that remarkable community. it has been a short debate, but we have heard some excellent and well-informed speeches. i was grateful for the kind things that the honourable gentleman, mr dimitrakopoulos, said about my time in hong kong. i agree with him about the importance of the freedom of speech and a vigorous media and, judging by my experiences last week, the media does remain – at least the written media – extremely vigorous. the honourable gentleman, mr watson, referred to the fact that we should not feel inhibited in what we say about hong kong or about other issues by our trading relationship with china. i am always surprised when people put the contrary point of view, not least because on the most recent figures, china had a eur 30 billion trade surplus with the european union. in the first six months of this year, the surplus was eur 20 billion and looks like becoming eur 40 billion for the whole year, so one needs to keep questions of trade in perspective, to put it very gently. it is a happy coincidence that the debate is taking place today, since i was in hong kong last week. it was my third visit since i relinquished the post of governor and my first full working visit as a commissioner. i can report first hand to the house that while, as several honourable gentlemen have pointed out, some problems exist, hong kong nevertheless remains a free society, undoubtedly one of the freest societies in asia as both the commission and parliament's annual reports make clear. the secret of hong kong's great success has always been its fusion of hong kong-chinese entrepreneurial culture with the attributes of a free and open society founded on the rule of law. the concept of one country, two systems enshrined in the joint declaration is designed to maintain that special mix – a mix between independent judiciary, clean civil service, zero tolerance of corruption, free press on the one hand and market economics on the other. as both our reports make clear, so far, on the whole, it is managing to maintain the mix and i share the view in the honourable gentleman's report that the fundamentals of the arrangement remain reasonably intact. we must remember that hong kong has been through a momentous few years. not only has it had to endure the transfer of sovereignty, no sooner did the flags change than hong kong – like the rest of asia – was hit by the financial crisis. it is a considerable tribute to the strength and resilience of people in hong kong that they have taken these tumultuous events in their stride. it does not surprise me entirely, they are many of them former refugees or the sons and daughters of refugees and they are used to handling and overcoming adversity. i am delighted that the hong kong economy is now bouncing back very strongly after a difficult couple of years. part of the reason for that is thanks to the excellent stewardship by hong kong's superb civil service, not least its chief secretary and financial secretary who served tung chee hwa with the same skill and dedication with which they served me. hong kong has an outstanding civil service and it would have been nice if that had been the thrust of the xinhwa report which the honourable gentleman referred to. for the most part, things have gone well but naturally there have been areas which have given rise to concern on the part of hong kong's many friends. both reports address these directly. in particular, both reports mention the judgments in the right of abode case earlier this year. that was an important matter. we have emphasised and will continue to stress that the way in which this matter was handled, in particular the involvement of beijing should remain wholly exceptional. there have been other issues too, including the recent controversy involving academic freedom and the even more recent one involving the public order ordinance, where the provisional legislature replaced the more liberal arrangement that my administration bequeathed with something rather less liberal. i make no comment on that but i find it reassuring that hong kong public opinion continues to make clear that it takes its freedoms and rights very seriously. that goes for the media as well, as i mentioned earlier. the long-term success of one country, two systems was always going to depend on the willingness of hong kong people to stand up for their rights and liberties; so far they have shown every sign of being determined to do so. there are one or two specific issues in our bilateral relationship to which both reports refer. one is visa-free access to the european union for hong kong sar passport holders and the same issues apply equally for macao. both the commission and the honourable gentleman's report support visa-free access. it was an issue that came up several times in my visit last week. i made clear then – as i make clear today – that i strongly support visa-free access. there are concerns on the part of some member states. these concerns are profoundly misplaced. they stem from worries about illegal immigration from mainland china, not from the special administrative regions of hong kong or macao. we remain wholly satisfied with the integrity of the border controls in place and with the integrity of sar passport issuing procedures. the council's visa working group which inspected the system was, i believe, impressed. i hope that we will be willing to grant visa-free access. i hope too that we will be able to reinforce a flourishing economic relationship with hong kong, with strengthened cooperation in other areas, such as education and the environment. we will aim to promote the use of the european communities' existing instruments in hong kong, such as participation in the fifth framework programme on research, as the honourable gentleman's report recommends. there are a number of further recommendations in the report which my officials will be studying closely. if i can just say one word about intellectual piracy, to which the honourable gentleman, mr de clercq, referred; it has been a problem for years. my administration took much more vigorous measures to deal with it. the present administration is taking particularly vigorous measures to deal with it, under the new director of the customs service whom i happen to know rather well because he was my private secretary for a number of years. everybody has been impressed by the leadership that he has shown in tackling what is undoubtedly a serious problem for industry. in closing, we strongly endorse the view in the parliament that this monitoring – your monitoring and our monitoring – is valuable and that it should continue. we do not seek to interfere in hong kong's affairs, but the international community and the european union in particular have a considerable stake in hong kong and its continuing rights and freedoms are embodied in a treaty lodged at the united nations. we will continue to maintain our interest in hong kong. it is a community which inspired admiration the world over for its achievements and which, much to the satisfaction of its last governor, continues to do so. (applause)
the commission's communication on the situation in central africa was published a year and a half ago. in the absence of consensus, it has been sitting in the council all this time. since then, an estimated 1.2 million people have been killed in the congolese war. in my report i am modestly trying to formulate a number of proposals to help end the spiral of violence and underdevelopment in which many of our acp partners have fallen. the commission has also proposed measures to prevent european funds from being misused to finance the war machine. my report can be summed up in three cs: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules are applied. more than ever before, there must be a clear link between financial assistance and debt relief on the one hand and good governance, democracy, respect for human rights and the rule of law on the other. i am even in favour of setting a ceiling on military expenditure in the acp countries above which aid and debt relief are no longer granted. if these conditions are not met, the european union must dare to take appropriate measures, ranging from diplomatic pressure and the suspension of aid to sanctions, with a preference for arms and diamond embargoes or so-called smart sanctions that affect the ruling elites and not the population. i would also like to urge the commission to use clear criteria for the suspension of aid. i believe it would be very useful to draw up a list of all possible infringements of the cotonou agreement under article 96. at the moment i cannot help feeling that the commission is taking too much of an ad hoc approach, which could lead to the reproach of double standards. in addition to conditionality, the emphasis is on greater coherence, greater coherence between the lomé convention and the cfsp and between the policies of the union and those of the member states. situations such as the one in ethiopia, where the european commission and some member states have frozen aid while other member states have launched new programmes, must be avoided. the commission has also been asked to present a report on the implementation of the budget. acp countries involved in armed conflicts must give the world bank and the imf full access to their accounts. where european development money is used to buy arms, structural aid must be suspended without delay. europe cannot be too strict in this respect. i think that all of this should not distract attention from one of the main tasks of the european union: the prevention of conflict. as rwanda and ethiopia have shown, there is usually no lack of alarms, but they are not always adequately and timely responded to. it is therefore important to tackle the causes of the problems at an early stage. conflict prevention also includes poverty reduction, strengthening democracy and civil society, better control of the illegal arms trade, the establishment of an african peace force, an international arrangement for the use of mercenaries and better control of the global ban on child soldiers. i am also pleading in my report, mr president, for greater involvement of the european parliament. i think it is essential that we have a greater say in the freezing or thawing of aid. i hope, mr president, that the council will not refer this important communication, which was inspired by the former commissioner deus pinheiro, back to the greek calendar. if we really want to contribute to a peace settlement in central africa, we must act now with force and coherence, we must make aid conditional and we must impose a total arms embargo on the great lakes region. under no circumstances should development funds be diverted to war. this is unacceptable, both for the people affected and for the european taxpayer. (applause from the left)
the committee on development and cooperation has adopted a report on the implementation of the convention on the rights of the child. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, on behalf of the committee on development and cooperation, i should like to add a few comments to the worthy points raised by mr van hecke as rapporteur. i can agree with him in every respect, on behalf of the whole committee, and i would also like to thank him for the constructive cooperation which has made it possible to incorporate all the amendments of the committee on development into the text, so that the president will be satisfied. he won't have to do a lot of voting. the concerns i wish to raise with you today, commissioner, are mainly practical. today we are still reading in our newspapers that things are going badly in côte d'ivoire, that our own people who are there to defeat the elections are in danger of being dragged into a new armed internal conflict. we are questioning the coherence of the policies of the commission and the council, whose absence i regret once again. while in september 1998 it was agreed not to provide budgetary aid to countries at war, in march 2000 the commission provided eur 110 million to rwanda, which occupies large parts of the democratic republic of congo, in defiance of the peace agreements. i know there are a lot of people in that conflict with butter on their heads. but are they not more stringent with the drc than with uganda? is it not more severe for zimbabwe than for rwanda? i have the impression that this is clearly the case, because in practice a policy of double standards is being applied to countries which are even involved in one armed conflict. some are supported almost unconditionally by the european union, the imf and others. other countries receive aid under strict conditions. this sometimes directly affects the balance of power between countries involved in the same conflict. let me be clear: i am against any form of budgetary aid to warring countries, because in most cases it is used directly or indirectly to buy arms. here is a second sore toe in our european policy making. the arms exports deserve mention here because of their absolute complicity in the various conflicts. it is not only the illegal arms trade that is flourishing, but the controlled arms trade is also more concerned with the 'fair competition' of arms dealers among themselves than with the peace we are advocating.
- mr president, i am personally not in favour of the death penalty for various reasons, but i would like to make two fundamental remarks here which depart from everything that has been said to me here. first of all, i think apples should not be compared to pears. there is a huge difference between the execution of the death penalty in, for example, the united states on the one hand, and in countries like china, pakistan, saudi arabia, north korea, to name just a few from a long line, on the other. firstly, it is the maximum penalty for the most serious criminals, who have also had numerous defence mechanisms in the democratic judicial system. in the other cases, it is usually a kind of random, gruesome intimidation against bystanders, not just serious common law criminals. whoever does all this in one fell swoop is committing peasant fraud. secondly, i must also say that in democratic countries it is easy to shed crocodile tears over the fate of serious criminals. it is a little more difficult to accept the fate of the bereaved, for example, of old people who are tortured to death for their savings, of poorly paid money couriers who are shot down by gangsters without a conscience, of children who are raped and murdered. if i were convinced that europe has the will to put the fate of the victims and their safety above all else, i would be here today with you and unreservedly condemn the death penalty. i can only observe in practice, especially in my own country, that the most serious criminals are systematically released early and not infrequently commit horrendous crimes again. it is not surprising that in these circumstances many people say that the death penalty for some repeat offenders could have saved many innocent lives.

in writing. - i am voting against the adoption of the charter of fundamental rights mainly because i do not want to be associated with a symbolic and media operation which aims to send a message of identity and hope to european public opinion when it is largely built on false pretenses. the consensual method adopted by the members of the convention has resulted, as is usual when unanimity is imposed, in a minimum charter whose wording is marked by significant gaps and ambiguities. the original idea of the convention was to have a consensual text which would enable the fifteen governments to make it binding by incorporation into the treaties. it is a fraudulent market that has finally prevailed: the text is weak and not binding. european citizens hardly need a solemn declaration on fundamental rights, which are already recognized by international conventions and national legal systems. i do not think that such a result will convince many people of the value of developing the european union. let 's move on . the charter is a bad exercise. too much symbolism and unanimity kills the politics. in order to make citizens feel involved in the european union, its policies and its institutions, it is particularly the responsibility of parliamentarians to reveal more about the crucial choices, contradictions, differences of interests and projects that animate economic and social actors on the european scene. the daily news - shipping or animal meal - tells us that much more regulation and control of markets and economic operators is needed and that established profit and short-term interests must be opposed. we need better european legislation to ensure a better quality of life for all and social solidarity. this is our priority fight to ensure that the affirmation of fundamental rights is concretized in political choices.

situation with de-greening and ageing do not make supplementary health insurance superfluous and in some cases even indispensable. this is further facilitated by the restructuring of social security everything to the individual citizen, we cannot expect everything from the government. the complementarity of collective solidarity mechanisms with private or group insurance is fully consistent with the rapporteur. - mr president, we are now dealing with a more mundane problem, namely the regularization of domestic work carried out by third parties in private households, because that is what we are talking about. the problem is that millions of people, especially women, are doing this work, and there is a very high demand for it, but there is no regulation of this work. the partial data available indicate that this is a very large sector. i have therefore tried to find out in different countries, but also at european level, how many people - as far as it is possible to find out, because it is often a case of undeclared work, of course - work in this sector. that's many millions. i have been looking in vain at european level for a comprehensive study that could give us an idea of the scale of the sector, the problems that exist in the sector, the arrangements, however incomplete, that exist in the european countries, the pilot projects that exist, the attempts that are being made, left and right, but i have to say that nothing could be found within the european commission. that is why i suggest to the commission: i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the question of the financial contribution of the european social fund to the structural funds. so i think both instruments are appropriate for a comprehensive study of the problem and to see what is being done in certain countries. so that is the first concrete question. that brings me to a second point. if the sector is to be regulated, a number of points must of course be examined. the first aspect is the matching of supply and demand. not only is the demand for domestic help increasing, but the supply of candidates is shrinking. who would want to work in that sector if you knew that it had to be done in black without social security and so on and so forth. furthermore, there is no single place where supply and demand can meet. so that's a first aspect. the second aspect is that the cost to the user, that is to say the private household who is providing a household help on the regular market, the white market, must not be significantly higher than the price on the black market, otherwise the black market will of course continue to be the rule. a third aspect is that the benefit to the worker, i.e. the domestic helper, must be better than the current benefits of undeclared work. fourthly, we must examine the social status that can be given to them. finally, all this will necessarily involve financial intervention by the public authorities, either fiscal or parafiscal, because a private family cannot pay for a full statute, even if it is limited, and then they all remain on the black market. i think that it is necessary to encourage the member states to regulate the sector through the employment guidelines, as has already been requested in the european parliament's opinion on these guidelines. so my question to the commission is: how far will the commission go to adapt the employment guidelines in the future, at least to refer to this service when it comes to services and to ask the member states to start looking for a solution in this area? and that brings me to the last point, the migrant women. that is, of course, a very specific problem. many migrant women are here illegally, come on tourist visas and go back every three months, for example to the eastern bloc countries, to renew that visa, and in the meantime they are employed here in a household. in some cases, they are exploited and abused. they have no help. they're afraid if they turn to anyone, their illegal situation will be known. that is why it is absolutely essential to pay special attention to this. i have noticed in recent weeks that there is a great deal of interest both from international trade union circles and from a number of national trade union organizations. the dutch have been with me, the belgians have been with me, the italians have also been with me on this problem. i would therefore recommend that the commission at least start looking for a solution.

- mr president, it is tempting to present the recent political events in côte d'ivoire as a victory of democracy over dictatorship, as a repetition of the belgrade scenario but this time in the heart of west africa. it's tempting, but it's wrong. because the reality is much more complex and the position of the new president is much more controversial. the fact that the junta leader, gueï, has not succeeded in his attempt to legitimize his power through rigged elections is a good thing. the european union and this parliament have made a major contribution to this by demanding that democratic elections be held and by introducing the suspension procedure. but that does not give laurent gbagdo, who won the elections, the legitimacy he needs. he too has played the nationalist card. he also supported the constitutional amendment, which was intended to eliminate his main opponent watara on the grounds that his parents are not both ivorian. the law had the effect of seriously disturbing the traditional ethnic harmony in côte d'ivoire. people of foreign origin, who make up between one third and one half of the population, have since been discriminated against and even attacked. the law also resulted in watara's supporters, mostly muslims from the north of the country, successfully boycotting the elections, with turnout not exceeding 40%, too low to give the new president a credible mandate. when watara's supporters took to the streets to demand new elections, their protest was violently crushed. muslim neighborhoods in the capital were attacked. at least 170 people were killed and 350 injured. fearing civil war, watara accepted the result. it would not be illogical if the european union today demanded new presidential elections in which all candidates could participate. but i admit there are a number of reasons not to do so. first, it would undermine the new president's efforts to restore national unity and reconciliation. in the context of this, he has offered his opponents several ministerial posts and declared 9 november a day of national mourning. on the other hand, all parties have now declared their willingness to cooperate in the restoration of the rule of law and peace. this must culminate, as some of my colleagues have already said, in the parliamentary elections on 10 december, in which all parties must be able to take part. i do not believe that europe should be adding fuel to the fire and further inflaming tensions when there is apparently a willingness to reconcile among the parties involved. but the real test for gbagdo will come on december 10th. we can only ensure that these elections are free and peaceful and hope that the new government of côte d'ivoire has the broad democratic base necessary to restore ethnic harmony and to help this country out of economic ruin.

commission. – mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like briefly to deal with the consequences of the ruling of the court of justice of the european communities in case c376/98 on the directive concerning the manufacture, presentation and sale of tobacco products, currently in second reading before this parliament. firstly, given the clear internal market objective to regulate the free movement of tobacco products in the community, article 95 of the treaty constitutes the appropriate legal basis for this proposal. secondly, there is no doubt that a better motivation of the effects of this provision on the internal market will strengthen it. in particular, there is a need to reinforce the motivation of the provisions of article 3 on carbon monoxide maximum authorised yields in the recitals of the directive. they should better reflect how they aim to eliminate obstacles to the free movement of goods or distortions of competition. finally, in relation to article 3, allowing manufacture in the european union only of products respecting this directive amounts to an export ban for products with higher tar, nicotine or carbon monoxide levels. the european parliament has therefore proposed to add article 133 dealing with the common commercial policy to article 95 as the legal basis. the commission could agree to this solution subject to the insertion of a specific article in the text of the directive which would explicitly regulate exports as such. this would be the case, for example, of an amendment on the transitional period to be applied to exports of cigarettes not complying with article 3 of the directive.
- mr president, when we have to choose between protecting the interests of the tobacco industry and defending public health in the context of harmonization measures, we make a quick decision: we go for public health. that is to say, we are working on lowering the maximum levels of tar, nicotine and carbon monoxide, then we are opting for adequate and prominent warnings and then we are banning misleading wording. we also want to see efficient arrangements for certain technical matters. despite this clear line of action, we must remember that we are a parliament of a union which is very much attached to the principles of the rule of law, and this means that we must recognize the limits of our competence, that we must know where the limits of the competence that we derive from article 95, the legal basis that we are invoking for the new tobacco directive, go. if i have read the judgment annulling the advertising directive correctly, it is necessary to examine whether there is a european market for tobacco products. it is then necessary to examine whether barriers to the internal market exist or are likely to arise. thirdly, it is necessary to examine whether the provisions we adopt will actually contribute to improving the functioning of the internal market and whether they are proportionate to the objective to be achieved. fourthly, we must examine whether the choices we are proposing in terms of content contribute to a high level of protection of public health, as we are also required to do in article 95. i do not see any problem with this directive in general. i am therefore very pleased with the answer i received in advance to a question which is also in our group, the answer given by the commissioner on how we should now deal with the proposal to introduce an export ban on products which we do not want to use ourselves, right away in this directive. if that can be settled in a legally binding way by adding a reference to the legal basis of article 133 of the treaty, then i think that is a solution which we should all support and i hope that we will not find the cancellation rights in our path afterwards. in any case, if it should turn out that we cannot get this export ban through sooner or later, we must at least make every effort to arrange it afterwards. for ethical reasons, no one in europe can accept that products which we do not think are good enough for us, products which we do not want europeans to consume, can be exported to, say, the africans. there's no way that could happen. so it's got to be settled. let us hope that we can do this now. president. - the next item is the report (doc. mrs martens was the spokeswoman for the epp group. she had good reason to be here a little later today. the commission is hoping to be in - mr president, commissioner, mr president-in-office, we do not consider the results of nice to be sufficient to prepare the eu for enlargement. the twelve enlargement countries prefer to talk about the unification of europe. that's saying something. we, many european citizens, grew up after the second world war in the hope that, once united, europe could play a role in the world for the sake of peace and the welfare of mankind, its own citizens and peoples. this dream is shining after nice, further than we had hoped. the first is that the treaty of nice was an attempt by the member states to reach a settlement behind closed doors on the remaining issues of amsterdam, with the primary concern of safeguarding their own interests. but it was not an effort to equip the eu to cope with enlargement. that has been said by many here this morning and i join them in full. as a representative of the parties united in the european free alliance, i would like to warn the commission, and especially the council, against the possibility that the eu may wish to be nothing more than an intergovernmental association. the member states preparing for accession will follow the example of the selfishness of our own member states. the regions and stateless peoples will also have to answer for their actions. they will calculate how much they can gain in scotland, in catalonia, in flanders if, instead of playing the federal game, they choose to become a member state themselves, with a guaranteed commissioner, guaranteed weight in the council, a greater number of seats than they can currently hold in the european parliament. should we then give up the dream of a united europe? i don't think he did. we are obliged to place our hopes in the post-nice process. we want this to be a real process in which the balance between the institutions of the union and those of the member states and the constituent regions must be defined with respect for subsidiarity.

mr president, to use the happy expression of the european group of authors' and composers' societies, the project we are discussing has been subjected to excessive attacks during its examination by the council. the compromise adopted by the council is therefore only a weak reflection of the commission's initial proposal, which was largely supported by the european parliament at first reading. the socialist group, like the vast majority of the committee on legal affairs and the internal market, was surprised by this attitude of the council of ministers, whose position contains several unacceptable elements, the most dangerous being the extension to 15 years of the transposition period by the member states when the commission's original proposal was already four years old. in principle, a delay of 15 years is unacceptable in a world as changing as ours, it implies the complete transformation of the sector, particularly due to technological progress. moreover, to create such a precedent at a time when the european union is about to double would send the wrong signal to the applicant countries. with such a delay, it would be better to abandon the idea of a directive. the socialist group therefore wanted parliament not to be disgraced by accepting proposals which ridicule the votes which took place at first reading. we are therefore glad, first of all, that this attitude has been understood and shared by the rapporteur, mr zimmerling, not only with regard to the problem of time, but also with regard to many other problems, those which he has listed this evening. we are glad, then, that almost all the democratic groups have shared the same constructive view. the debate, and above all the vote that will follow, will therefore once again be between those who want to move forward in the construction of europe and those who are being held back by national interests, whose legitimacy seems highly doubtful, as opposed to the interests of the european creators. moreover, the need for a common position on the subject before us is all the greater because only a common position will enable the european union to exert its influence at international level so that the interests of creators can be defended at world level.
- madam president, i can reassure you and mr simpson. it is not my intention to decimate the postal services. on the contrary, it is intended to make them better and i do not think that mr ferber's report is a good proposal. some of my group do not share my views on a number of points. you'll hear about it later. i think the commission's proposal is a good one. it is an important step towards a single market, important for consumers, for businesses that use postal services, but we have closed the door. if we continue at our present rate, it will take 100 years to achieve an open market for postal services in europe. i have not seen in sweden, mr simpson, what you are saying, and they have an open market. i don't hear that from the dutch. and when i talk to other people in the mailroom in private, i don't hear that there either. so i think what you're saying is partly true and what you like to hear. i have tabled an amendment to at least have an end date. my amendment, i think, is gradual and controlled. it's not as ambitious as i would have liked, but i think it's a good compromise. what we are facing is a cynical situation. we follow the post-monopolies. but what do i see? the german post goes to the stock exchange, the mecca of capitalism, advertises in my country in the newspapers to say, buy our shares, because we are a profitable company. and where does that profit come from? out of the monopoly. what i see is that the royal mail has shares in a postal company in sweden because it can compete there on the open market. can one swede in britain compete with it? no, i'm not going. that's the kind of situation we protect. we're protecting big business in this case. they literally dictated the law to us. i think it is a hypocritical situation, because in the meantime these companies are buying up everywhere, preparing for the free market. they do so very actively. they restructure, they lay off people, mr simpson, even in the monopoly and i think we protect that. we have made the safety net a hammock for the users of the post office, where those big postal companies can lie down and do what they can continue to do. i think that's a bad thing. i shall vote against that.
- the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to noise and the introduction of a european noise warning system; noise expressed in decibels is a measure of living standards under this directive. that is primarily the merit of the rapporteur and the support he has received in the committee. this framework directive has the ambition of combating noise pollution for european citizens. there are some good and important things in this directive: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the information provided by the member states. but let me say something about the hackneyed work and the struggle that has been carried out in the wings, in the committee next door, below, above, lately and just now here. the rapporteur has set out in his proposal a number of indicators, a number of objectives concerning airports. i understand him in that. in my country, too, it's a very hot item. and in my country, too, the public is awakened by it. it is our duty as members of the european parliament and as a european institution to do something about this. i also want clear objectives, absolutely, but the standards proposed by the rapporteur are not being met at the moment. perhaps another group will say that there are technical problems. you name it. they're just not fully formed yet. we must give the commission time, but not too much time, to do something about it. that is why i think the amendments tabled by my colleague, mr bernd lange, are so important. i hope that they will be supported and that the commission will give clear and well-founded standards within eight months, because that is what is at stake and i hope that we can reach a compromise on that too in this house.
commission. – i would like to thank the rapporteur; mr de roo, for his work. environmental noise reduces the health and quality of life of at least 25% of the eu population. it causes illnesses such as stress and increased blood pressure as well as reductions in children's learning capacity. aviation noise probably has the highest profile today in europe, while locally noise from road traffic and sources such as trains and construction work is usually the biggest problem. what should we do in order to deal with this growing problem? it was tempting to use this directive and other directives to set new eu noise limit values as soon as possible. that may seem the logical way to control the level of noise but it would be premature to set new limit values in this way for two main reasons. firstly, because we already have market access legislation which set limit values for many road vehicle types and for equipment used outdoors. it would be simpler and quicker to tighten the noise standards in the existing legislation than to introduce new daughter directives. we will consider what these standards needs to be once we have the evidence from strategic noise maps. secondly, this proposal uses a new approach to assess the overall noise situation in europe in terms of numbers of people who are disturbed by noise. it is impossible at the moment, because we use such a large variety of indicators and assessment methods, to characterise noise in different member states. a particular problem is the noise around airports. because of commercial competitiveness between airports and between aviation manufacturers it has not been possible to obtain correct and verified noise levels. getting comparable information about the noise around airports will be a big step forward. the commission would therefore not be able to support the amendments introducing airport limit values at this stage. once member states have provided the comparable information then the commission will consider the question of community limit values and other measures to reduce the numbers of people affected by noise and report back to the council and parliament. as a general means of improving the situation, we propose that authorities in member states should produce action plans in close consultation with the public. they should outline any measures that member states take or plan to take to reduce the noise problem. we are not prescribing what measures should be taken. this is for the member states to decide. however, the action plans must be published, even if all they say is that no improvements are planned, so that people living locally will know the situation and will be able to discuss the issue with their elected representatives. i am grateful to members for their amendments strengthening the text and public participation, which we wholeheartedly accept. our first priority is to produce strategic noise maps using standardised indicators and assess all types of noise to give us an accurate picture of the number of people who are suffering and where. our first report on the directive is due to be submitted to parliament in 2007. this will include a careful consideration of limit values. a five-yearly review process will then ensure that members have a continued input. we value your input and welcome the majority of amendments you have contributed today. i hope we can now agree a positive way forward with a view to achieving a common position with the council next week. as far as the amendments are concerned, the commission has considered 49 amendments and is able to accept 25 of them fully or in part. the commission can accept amendments nos 1 to 3, 14, 18, 27, 30, 34, 35 and 43. the following amendments are acceptable in principle or in part: amendments nos 6, 7, 12, 13, 17, 21, 22, 25, 28, 29, 32, 39, 42, 44 and 45. the commission cannot accept the other amendments. to conclude i would like to thank european parliament for the detailed consideration it has given to this proposal and for the many helpful amendments which have been contributed.
mr president, i think that the commission has a duty to make a statement on the situation in the community. there is only one amendment, but it is an important one. let me explain why. we have adopted five amendments at first reading, two of which are essential. the first was to prohibit the use of ethylhydroxyethylcellulose - i apologize for the scientific expression which is a bit complex, but it is e 467 - as a new food additive. the council has followed us, which is very good. however, it did not follow us on another amendment which refused to extend the authorization of another additive: sodium alginate (e 401) for unprocessed prepackaged, peeled or cut carrots. it seems a little trivial, but i will explain why the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has done well in proposing to the house that this authorization should not be accepted. first, there are three criteria which generally govern the acceptance or rejection of a new additive. the first criterion is technological need. the technological need here is hardly in dispute. it is worth noting that sodium alginate is used as a strengthening agent for peeled, cut or packaged carrots ready for consumption. it thus prevents the pieces from softening and helps to maintain the organoleptic qualities of the carrots. the second criterion is the usefulness to the consumer. it is understood that treatment with alginate may mislead the consumer by making a product appear fresh when it is not. furthermore, additives cannot generally be used for unprocessed foodstuffs - as is the case here - which have not undergone any treatment leading to a substantial change in their original state. so there is a second deception and that is why we cannot say that there is no benefit to the consumer but on the contrary a nuisance to the consumer. the third criterion is safety. according to all the scientific literature and the scientific committee for food, this additive is harmless but has a laxative effect, which is considered negligible at the doses used. the number of additives with laxative effects may be a problem. the synergistic effect of all these laxative additives has not been evaluated. there are many of them, and i would remind you that in 1992 the scientific committee for food recommended that a study be made of the contribution of food components with potential laxative effects to the overall dietary burden of substances with this biological property. that study was never done. it would therefore be appropriate as a precautionary measure - before this study has been carried out - not to authorise new additives with this property. those are the three criteria. two of them are not satisfactory. i think it is perfectly correct to refuse authorization of this additive. it is not a question of being too rigid about an additive, but of being rigorous. there are currently 307 food additives on the european market, 163 of which are completely harmless. it's not bad. others, while not dangerous, may present a potential health hazard, especially when used together. it is always about synergy issues. we know that the commission is preparing a new directive on additives. i think it would be quite interesting to show what parliament's position is on the occasion of the revision of the directive so that the additive burden is less than it is today. i think that would be good for public health without any damage to industry.
- mr president, we voted with the epp group in the committee on the environment in favour of the lannoye report, and it is also in full accordance with our wishes. i shall therefore suggest to my colleagues that they support it, including the one amendment. we are not a party to peeled and shredded carrots dipped in alginate. we support the rapporteur's amendment. we'd rather not see that coming. mr president, at this late hour and with a piece of carrot in my throat, i do not think i have much to say to my colleagues. i think i have made our group's position clear.
the rapporteur. - the president, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the discussion of this report on the organization of official controls in the field of animal nutrition can be compared to the final stage of a ride on a bicycle. i believe, ladies and gentlemen, that the adoption of the joint text will ensure that we win this round tomorrow. but that does not mean, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, that the yellow jersey is in or that the final victory is in sight. as i explained in the second reading, the basis of this amendment to the directive is a crisis. a few years ago it became clear that dioxin-contaminated citrus pulp from brazil had been introduced into animal feed in the netherlands, belgium and germany. it also showed that the commission did not have the right tools to take appropriate action. that, ladies and gentlemen, is what we are going to set right. i have seen in the trialogue how the governments of the member states put their own interests ahead of the public interest. the french presidency was courteous but tough. i did everything i could to ensure that the european commission could act independently in urgent cases, and especially where the health of humans and animals was at risk, without having to consult the member states. the measures adopted by the commission shall apply immediately and shall also be submitted to the standing committee on animal nutrition within 10 days. the negotiations also led to the conclusion - and i regard this as a victory for this parliament - that the safeguard measures adopted by the commission remain in force until they are replaced by another legislative act. a second problem was that the member states did not understand our demand to allow unannounced checks in this way. it was clear during the trialogue that the council was not going to give in. i have therefore received a very clear hint from the french presidency, a very clear message, a message which was one of 'no passaran'. unannounced inspections are clearly considered by the member states as an infringement of their sovereignty, but the member states have shown their willingness to provide the commission's experts with all the assistance necessary for the performance of their inspection tasks. the results of the checks will also be communicated to the european parliament. fellas, i think i pulled out what i could from the fire. i believe that our parliament is considerably tightening up the council's common position and i recommend that the common text be adopted. but as i was just saying, we're ending up here in a sort of a bicycle tour. there's already a new ride on the schedule for tomorrow. then we have our colleague marit paulsen of the liberal group, who will have the opportunity to tighten up the directive even further. she deserves all our support for that, and i wish her every success, and she knows she can count on me.
- mr president, i entirely agree with what mr atkins says, but to my surprise i see in the list that amendment no 62, which i tabled with 31 other members and for which i telephoned the services three times to say that it is my amendment, that it is still under the name of sir robert atkins. i think it is very strange, because i think that somehow it affects the debate. i don't think that's allowed. it is clearly an amendment tabled by me with 31 members and i would therefore like to have it in my name. i think that if i make a few phone calls about it, it should be possible to correct it. i'm sorry, but i don't think this is a normal way of working.
- mr president, i wonder if we can adopt a resolution on the nice summit at all. i am a little afraid that we have found ourselves in an american situation of a new census. i am not saying that the european parliament should be the only institution in the world to have a right to vote, but i am saying that it should be the only institution in the world to have the right to vote. i quote: annex 3 to the draft treaty states that the threshold of 73.4% of the votes, which changes with the accession of new member states, must be reached once all the candidates have acceded. that means 253 votes in the council of ministers. but that also means that the blocking minority - and that is what the nice agreement was about - is 93. but what do we read one sentence later? 91 votes! i know. you realize that it is difficult for us to talk about a treaty if it contains such errors.
- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, last year, on 25 december, that is to say, on christmas day, three prominent kosovo albanian citizens went on a three-week hunger strike in brussels to call the attention of the international community to the plight of these prisoners and, in particular, to the many thousands of missing persons. in mid-january this year, several thousand kosovars demonstrated in front of the council building in brussels and a delegation was received by the services of mr solana and the portuguese presidency. solana, we were told, understood these demands, but we were also told, and i quote, that it was difficult to do business with a regime like milosevic's. ladies and gentlemen, in the meantime milosevic has been replaced by kostunica and i was in kosovo when kostunica was received very warmly at the biarritz summit and millions of euros of aid were poured in unconditionally, but what has kostunica proved in the meantime on the prisoners' problem? oh yes, one of the symbolic prisoners, mrs flora brovina, was released, but we cannot stop there, ladies and gentlemen. our compromise resolution is clear in paragraphs 1, 2 and 3. we want the political prisoners to be released quickly and an impartial inquiry into the fate of the missing to be launched. mr solana, mr patten, i ask you both to put sufficient pressure on the kostunica regime to comply with these demands. i would like to say to mr kostunica: you would gain credibility by using the coming christmas to commit such an act. it can only be good for stability in the region.
